_(Source: **Ramp AI Index** — monthly median AI spend per employee, three cohorts, **Aug-2023 → Jul-2026, 36 observations**. **CSV supplied by Felipe 2026-08-20**; on disk at [`../_data/ramp-ai-index/spend_per_employee.csv`](../_data/ramp-ai-index/spend_per_employee.csv) (raw, verbatim) + `series.json` (derived: MoM, 3-/12-mo compound, y/y, regime rates). Rebuild: `py _wiki/_tools/build_ramp_index.py`. ⚠️ **Ramp's own methodology note is NOT verified** — the cohort definitions are read off the column headers, i.e. "top 10% / top 1%" taken as the MEDIAN spend per employee WITHIN that cohort. Until now this page cited the index only second-hand, via Goldman and MS.)_

- 🔴 **THE PRIMARY SERIES, AND IT IS STRONGER THAN THE SECOND-HAND VERSION.** Every number below is off the file, not a note about the file:

| Cohort | Aug-23 | Jul-26 | 3-yr multiple | Compound, full period | Trailing 12-mo | Down months |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| All companies (median) | $2.32 | $11.95 | **5.15x** | +4.79%/mo | +8.38%/mo | 7 / 35 |
| Top 10% cohort | $62.02 | **$650.00** | **10.48x** | +6.94%/mo | +10.75%/mo | 8 / 35 |
| Top 1% cohort | $858.59 | **$7,400.50** | **8.62x** | +6.35%/mo | +12.08%/mo | 11 / 35 |

- 🔴🔴 **THE GROWTH RATE ITSELF IS ACCELERATING — THREE REGIMES, NOT ONE TREND.** Compound %/mo within each 12-month window (median / top-10% / top-1%): **Sep-23–Aug-24 +2.25 / +4.17 / +3.65 ➜ Sep-24–Aug-25 +3.78 / +5.48 / +2.83 ➜ Sep-25–Jul-26 +8.79 / +11.72 / +13.46.** The median company's monthly growth rate has **near-quadrupled**; **63% of the median cohort's entire three-year dollar gain (74% for the top 1%) landed in the last six months.** This is the cleanest bottom-up evidence on the page that the spend inflection is real and *broadening* — a top percentile at +13.5%/mo is a story about a handful of AI-native firms, but the **median** company tripling its rate is adoption widening. Its down-months also thinned: **5 in year one ➜ 1 in each of the last two years**, and only three months in three years saw all three cohorts fall together (**Dec-23, Aug-24, Nov-25**).
- ✓ **GOLDMAN'S BASIS IS NOW PINNED — "start of the year" = the DEC-25 PRINT, not January.** GS (2026-08-14) gave *"$5 at the start of the year ➜ $12 in July"* and *"top decile $240 ➜ $650"*. Against the file, **$5 and $240 both match Dec-25 exactly (median $5.26, top-10% $241.46)**; January is $5.86 / $281.23. So the move is **+127% median / +169% top-decile on the Dec-25 base** (+104% / +131% on a January base) — against **+140% / +171% implied by GS's rounded pair.** The page's "more than doubled YTD" claim **holds on either base**; the magnitude does not, so quote the base.
- ⚠️ **THE MS y/y PERCENTAGES CARRIED ON [[SPCX]] DO NOT REPRODUCE OFF THIS SERIES.** MS's **levels** tie well — median *"~$11 in June"* vs $10.93 ✓, top 1% *">$4,880"* vs $4,973.62 ✓, top decile *"$516"* vs $520.48 (0.9% off, rounding or a restated vintage). The **growth rates do not**: the file gives **June y/y of +154.8% median, +215.4% top-decile, +200.9% top-1%**, against MS's *"+167%"* and *"each +~240-250%"*. Note the top decile's **+240.6% is the JULY y/y** — so the exhibit reads like June levels paired with July growth, or MS's 2025 base was lower than this file's (i.e. Ramp restated 2025 upward). **Unresolved; use the file's rates.**
- ⚠️⚠️ **THE "06-18 CUT" (median $11 / top-10% $611) IS NOT ON THIS SERIES AT ALL.** On 2026-06-18 the newest available print would have been **May-26 (median $10.50 / top-10% $465.39)**. **$611 appears in no month** of the top-10% series — the nearest value is Jul-26's $650.00, a month that did not exist on 06-18 — and "median $11" matches Jun/Jul-26, not May. So that mark is either **a different Ramp cut** (a mean rather than a median, or "top 10%" read as the 90th-percentile boundary instead of the median within the decile) **or a transcription error.** This **partly supersedes reconciliation entry #8** (`../_meta/reconciliation-2026-08-17-night.md`), which judged all three readings to be "one series still climbing, **not** three conflicting readings": the **GS chain checks out against primary, the 06-18 mark does not.**
- **PRACTICAL RULE going forward:** quote **levels and compound rates off `series.json`**; treat any second-hand y/y on this index as unverified until it reproduces. And do not read the index as a revenue proxy — the panel is Ramp's US corporate-card / bill-pay customer base, the cohorts are **re-cut monthly** (so part of each cohort's step is re-ranking, not same-company growth — top-1% monthly σ is 16.4pp vs the median's 5.7pp), and **per-employee is a ratio** a hiring freeze can flatter. ⚠️ Jul-26's top decile prints **exactly $650.00** — a round number to the cent in a two-decimal series reads as rounded or preliminary, so that +24.9% month is the softest datapoint in the file.

Consequences, both ways: throughput improvements (e.g. from prefill/decode disaggregation) convert DIRECTLY into revenue on the same infrastructure footprint — UBS models GM ~56% exiting C27 and ~60% exiting C28 and says this could accelerate the path to 60%. But the symmetric risk, which UBS does not state, follows immediately: at a fixed price per million tokens for 12 months, the SELLER absorbs every adverse cost move inside the term — including CS-4's DOUBLED power draw.** **This is the first token-priced supply contract on this page whose margin is explicitly a function of hardware efficiency rather than of list-price changes.**

- **Latency as a product, not a cost line — three customer statements from the Cerebras event that are useful because they describe what speed BUYS rather than what it saves:** **[[OPENAI]]** said Ultrafast "removes the traditional tradeoff between model intelligence and response speed," that engineers already use it for high-priority workloads, that OpenAI **wants Ultrafast to become the DEFAULT user experience**, and Cerebras confirmed **GPT-5.6 Sol already runs on Cerebras systems today**; **Cognition** — coding models at ~1,000 tokens/sec, specialised models at several thousand, cutting agent completion times; **[[CRWD]]** — lower latency "allows more security analysis within the same DECISION WINDOW."
- **Monetisation, concrete: ChatGPT ADS are rolling out to European markets — Germany, France, Spain, Italy, Sweden, Norway, Denmark, the Netherlands, Austria** (@rustybrick, 2026-08-19).

Patel's countervailing observation from Ep. 25 is that the practical moat is "the harness, interface and accumulated user trust", and that staff "sometimes abandon an open model after ONE poor experience" — a real adoption friction, but one that erodes with each release.**


## Recent signals (Aug 18, /wiki-ingest) — **"OPEN WEIGHTS" ARE NOT FREE, AND AI MARGINAL COST IS A DISTRIBUTION, NOT ONE NUMBER**

**Ben Thompson splits inference into two businesses.** A simple consumer query can cost only marginally more than serving a web page; a test-time-scaled task that reasons for hours or days carries direct, rising marginal cost. Open weights remove the model-builder's R&D charge, not the cost of running inference, so the relevant comparison is all-in cost per completed task. That heterogeneity explains three downstream business-model moves: consumer AI gravitates to advertising; heavy enterprise users force usage pricing beyond a seat bundle; and routers/down-tiering become structural rather than tactical. It also sharpens the frontier-lab question: self-improvement can lower cost-to-serve and widen a lead, but only if revenue compounds before the capital curve runs out (Ben Thompson, *Invest Like the Best*, 2026-08-18).
## 🆕 Recent signals (Aug 17, /run-inbox) — 🔴 **THE VERTICAL-INTEGRATION THREAT TO FRONTIER-LAB API REVENUE IS NOW A THREE-POINT MARGIN LADDER (−500% ➜ 25% ➜ 40%), THE TWO BIGGEST OPEN-MODEL PUBLISHERS OF 2026 ARE THE *CHIP MAKERS*, AND ENTERPRISE AI SPEND PER EMPLOYEE HAS MORE THAN DOUBLED YTD WHILE ONLY 2% OF THE S&P CAN QUANTIFY AN EARNINGS IMPACT**

**① THE SHARPEST TOKEN-ECONOMICS DATAPOINT OF THE RUN, AND IT IS AN APPLICATION-LAYER BUYER WALKING AWAY FROM FRONTIER APIs (Deutsche Bank · Edison Yu, "Double clicking on Cursor" — [[SPCX]] company update, 2026-08-17).**
- 🔴 **THE LADDER, WORKED AT ~$120/MONTH OF MODEL USAGE AT PUBLISHED API RATES — all three cases carried because the sequence is the argument:**

| Case | Revenue to Cursor | COGS (model spend) | Gross profit | GM |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| **A** — old uncapped Pro seat | $20 | **$120 paid to [[ANTHROPIC]] or [[OPENAI]]** | −$100 | **−500%** |
| **B** — today's Team seat + usage meter | $160 | $120 | $40 | **25%** |
| **C** — Cursor Router sends **2/3 of agent turns** to Composer 2.5 | $80 | **$48** | $32 | **40%** |

- 🔴 **THE COEFFICIENT UNDER IT: DB estimates Cursor's INTERNAL Composer model costs just 10–20% OF CLAUDE/GPT** — so an application-layer buyer moving two-thirds of agent turns to an in-house model takes gross margin from −500% through 25% to 40%, i.e. **the same end-user workload takes the frontier lab's take from $120 to $48.** The original margin problem was the flat-fee structure (pre-June-2025 Pro allowed 500 premium requests/month, so heavy users burned inference against fixed revenue); the dollar-denominated usage pool fixed the *pricing*, and the router fixed the *cost*.
- **The buyer's scale, which is why this is not an anecdote: CURSOR AT ~$1BN ARR WITH ABOUT 300 PEOPLE**, DB-estimated opex only **~$200–300m** (opex is "the inverse of COGS: extremely light"). DB names **Claude Code and OpenAI Codex** as Cursor's two most direct competitors — and Cursor now sits **inside** [[SPCX]] (1.4 GW of owned compute exiting 2Q26, >2 GW planned by year-end), so the router's owner also owns the substitute model *and* the metal.
- ➜ **How this page should hold it: this is the FIRST fully-quantified instance of the theme's central bear — that the app layer captures the margin by substituting cheaper in-house inference for frontier tokens. It is not a price cut, not a discount, and not a demand fall; it is MIX. ⚠️ Magnitude unquantified: DB publishes no dollar figure for Cursor's spend with either lab and no Anthropic/OpenAI split, and the 10–20% ratio plus the two-thirds routing share are DB ESTIMATES rather than disclosures.**

**② WHERE INFERENCE TOKENS ACTUALLY GET SERVED — AND THE ANSWER IS SMALL MODELS ON LOCAL RUNTIMES, NOT FRONTIER APIs (Hugging Face · Adina Yakefu / Apolinário / Irene Solaiman, "State of Open Models: Summer 2026 Observations", 2026-08-14).**
- 🔴 **THE DEMAND SHAPE, AND IT IS THE MOST IMPORTANT NUMBER ON THIS PAGE FOR TOKEN-MIX FORECASTS: MODELS UNDER 1B TAKE 83% OF ALL-TIME DOWNLOADS AND EVERYTHING ABOVE 100B TAKES 1%; IN 2026 DOWNLOADS, ONLY 3% OF VOLUME GOES TO MODELS ABOVE 70B.** Attention and adoption are close to disjoint: **of the top 25 repos by 2026 downloads and the top 25 by likes, EXACTLY ONE appears in both**; **no model published in 2026 reaches the download top 25**, while **13 of the 25 date from 2022** (all-MiniLM-L6-v2 pulled **1.55bn times in seven months** against 5,156 likes).
- 🔴 **THE PUBLISHER LEAGUE TABLE IS THE STRATEGIC ITEM: "The two organizations publishing the most new open models this year are also the companies making the hardware: AMD and NVIDIA. Each released more than 200 new model repositories, far ahead of the rest of the field, with LiquidAI ranking third at around 100. HARDWARE VENDORS HAVE REALIZED THAT OPEN MODELS ARE A WAY TO SELL CHIPS."** ➜ Read against this page's standing "a token is a token / margin transfers to the infrastructure layer" thread: the chip vendors are now **funding the token-price deflation themselves**, because cheap open weights pull volume onto their silicon. [[AMD]] and [[NVDA]] as the #1 and #2 open-model publishers of 2026 is the cleanest evidence yet that the deflation is a deliberate hardware-demand strategy rather than a competitive accident.
- **The runtime layer is growing 3–7x faster than the modelling core, which is where "where does the token get served" is actually being decided: repos declaring the gguf library ROSE 464%, lerobot 194% and Apple's mlx 148%, against 16% for transformers/peft and 21% for diffusers.** The **ggml team joined Hugging Face in February** (project stays open-source and community-governed), and the July snapshot carries GGUF builds of **DeepSeek-V4-Flash (~284B) and KIMI-K3 (~2.8 TRILLION)** — "local inference used to mean an 8B model on a laptop. It now means a **trillion-parameter mixture-of-experts spread across a few consumer machines**." ➜ **A trillion-parameter MoE that runs off-API is a structural ceiling on how much of the token pool the frontier labs can ever meter.**
- **Hub scale for context: public model repos 2.43m ➜ 2.96m (+21.5%) Jan→Aug 2026, datasets 711k ➜ 1m, Spaces 1.00m ➜ 1.44m — but the distribution is extreme: ~85.6% of models have FEWER THAN 200 LIFETIME DOWNLOADS and 1.5% OF REPOSITORIES ACCOUNT FOR 99.2% OF ALL DOWNLOADS.** China's monthly parameter ceiling ran **754B to 2.78 TRILLION** while US models stayed under 130B in five of seven months.
- 🔴 **LICENSING IS STARTING TO TURN, WHICH IS THE FIRST SIGN THE FREE TIER IS BEING MONETIZED: of 178 Chinese releases above 20B this year, 59% ARE APACHE 2.0 AND 22% MIT (vs 29% Apache/MIT on the American side of the same band — 41% custom, 30% undeclared) — BUT KIMI K3 AND QWEN3.8 HAVE BEGUN ADDING NON-COMMERCIAL RESTRICTIONS AND REVENUE-SHARE REQUIREMENTS, which HF reads as the start of a shift toward clearer monetization paths.** ➜ If the cheap-open-weights tier starts charging, the price floor this page has been treating as ~zero moves up — the single most important thing to watch for the "commoditization is permanent" leg of the debate.

**③ THE ENTERPRISE-ADOPTION REALITY CHECK, AND IT CUTS BOTH WAYS: SPEND IS INFLECTING WHILE MEASURED EARNINGS IMPACT IS STILL ~NIL (Goldman Sachs, "What Q2 earnings reports signaled about the state of corporate AI adoption", US Weekly Kickstart, 2026-08-14).**
- 🔴 **THE SPEND INFLECTION — RAMP AI INDEX: median company monthly AI spend per employee rose from $5 AT THE START OF THE YEAR TO $12 IN JULY; the TOP-DECILE company spends $650/MONTH/EMPLOYEE in July vs $240 at the start of the year.** ✓ **This RECONCILES with the marks already on this page rather than contradicting them: Ramp's 06-18 cut had the median at $11 and the top-10% at $611 — so $12 / $650 in July is the same series two months later, still climbing.** ~7% of S&P 500 companies discussed AI implementation expenses.
- **AND THE LEVEL IS STILL TINY, WHICH IS THE BULL POINT: AI inference expenses are estimated at LESS THAN 0.5% OF S&P 500 REVENUES** (built from leading model providers' H1-2026 revenues × the estimated S&P 500 share), far below COGS/SG&A/labour; **89% of GS IT-survey respondents put AI at 1–5% OF TOTAL IT BUDGETS.** ➜ Demand is early, not saturated — the direct counter to the "token optimization = the top" reading of the 08-10 phase-change block above.
- 🔴 **THE FUNDING MIX IS THE PART WITH MARKET-STRUCTURE CONSEQUENCES: ROUGHLY TWO THIRDS OF COMPANIES FUND AI INFERENCE COSTS BY REALLOCATING FROM OTHER SOURCES — 18% FROM SOFTWARE BUDGETS AND 11% FROM LABOUR.** GS's own read: reallocation from software **redistributes earnings within the market** (a zero-sum transfer from SaaS to the AI stack), while reallocation from labour is the **macro** worry. ➜ This is the mechanism by which token spend shows up as somebody else's revenue decline, and it is the first published split of it on this page.
- ⚠️ **THE MEASURED RETURN IS STILL ABSENT: only 11% OF S&P 500 COMPANIES QUANTIFIED THE IMPACT OF AI PRODUCTIVITY ON A SPECIFIC USE CASE (coding, customer support) AND JUST 2% QUANTIFIED AN IMPACT ON EARNINGS — both flat vs Q1 2026 — and the difference in earnings growth between the quantifiers and the rest of the index was "modest and STATISTICALLY INSIGNIFICANT."** GS economists still cite a **20–30% labour-productivity uplift in the limited areas where genAI has been deployed** — i.e. real gains, in a small footprint, not yet visible in EPS.
- **COUNTER-EVIDENCE ON THE SOFTWARE-DISRUPTION LEG, which this page has been carrying as an open question since the 08-10 "build their own" checks: only 17% OF RESPONDENTS EXPECT TO BUILD MORE SOFTWARE rather than buy from packaged vendors, and ARR growth for the median stock across 39 GS-COVERED SOFTWARE NAMES HAS ACCELERATED SLIGHTLY in recent quarters.** GS's named exception is an anecdote, not a trend: **Starbucks reportedly developing in-house AI tools to replace some external applications from the likes of Microsoft and IBM.** ➜ **Logged as the anecdote GS itself frames as the exception — not filed on any company page.**

## 🆕 Recent signals (Aug 15, /run-inbox) — 🔴 **THE CACHE DISCOUNT IS NOT A VENDOR CHOICE, IT IS AN INDUSTRY CONVENTION: [[OPENAI]], [[ANTHROPIC]] AND [[GOOG]] ALL PRICE A CACHED TOKEN AT EXACTLY 10% OF A COMPUTE TOKEN**

- 🔴 **THE EXHIBIT, AND IT IS THE PRICE SIGNAL THAT MAKES KV-CACHE OFFLOAD TO NAND ECONOMICALLY RATIONAL.** *"There is a meaningful cost difference between compute (fresh uncached) tokens and cached tokens… Because accessing cached data is far less computationally intensive than recomputing it, cache tokens are significantly cheaper to serve. **The industry has converged on a 90% discount for cache reads — OpenAI, Anthropic and Google all price cached token at exactly 10% of compute token on current-generation models.**"* (Bernstein · Mark C. Newman / April Li / Phoebe Sun, "Memory & Storage: Why Sandisk investor day makes us more bullish on SNDK," 2026-08-14, Exhibit 4 — sourced to the vendors' own public pricing pages.)

| Model | Compute ($/1M tok) | Cache read ($/1M tok) | Cache as % of input |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| OpenAI gpt-5.6-sol | $5.00 | $0.50 | **10.0%** |
| OpenAI gpt-5.6-terra | $2.00 | $0.20 | **10.0%** |
| OpenAI gpt-5.6-luna | $0.20 | $0.02 | **10.0%** |
| OpenAI gpt-5.4 | $2.50 | $0.25 | **10.0%** |
| _OpenAI gpt-4o (prior gen)_ | _$2.50_ | _$1.25_ | _50.0%_ |
| Claude Opus 5 / 4.8 | $5.00 | $0.50 | **10.0%** |
| Claude Sonnet 5 | $2.00 | $0.20 | **10.0%** |
| Claude Sonnet 4.6 | $3.00 | $0.30 | **10.0%** |
| Claude Haiku 4.5 | $1.00 | $0.10 | **10.0%** |
| Gemini 3.1 Pro (≤200k) | $2.00 | $0.20 | **10.0%** |
| Gemini 3 Flash | $0.50 | $0.05 | **10.0%** |
| Gemini 2.5 Pro (≤200k) | $1.25 | $0.13 | **10.0%** |
| Gemini 2.5 Flash | $0.30 | $0.03 | **10.0%** |
| DeepSeek V4-Pro | $0.44 | ~$0.00 | **~0.8%** |
| DeepSeek V4-Flash | $0.14 | ~$0.00 | **~2%** |

- ➜ **WHY THIS MATTERS TO THIS THEME SPECIFICALLY, AND IT CUTS BOTH WAYS.** (1) **The 10% convention is a floor under the storage thesis:** at a 90% discount, re-serving a cached token is worth ~9x its price in avoided compute, which is exactly the arbitrage that justifies persisting KV cache to NAND rather than recomputing it. It converts "KV cache offload" from an engineering preference into a priced decision, and it is the demand-side complement to the [[SNDK]] KV-cache TAM on [[hbm-memory]]. (2) **But it is also a token-DEFLATION mechanism of precisely the kind this page tracks:** every workload that shifts from compute tokens to cached tokens takes a **90% price cut** on that volume. On a revenue-per-token basis, cache adoption looks exactly like the "token optimization" phase change logged Aug 10 — spend holds only if cached volume grows more than 10x the compute volume it displaces.
- **The generational pattern is the tell, and it is worth watching as a leading indicator:** the only model in the table NOT at 10% is **prior-generation gpt-4o at 50%**. ➜ **The discount DEEPENED with the current generation (50% → 10%), i.e. the labs are actively pricing to push traffic into cache. That is a deliberate migration, not a passive one — and it should accelerate the KV-cache storage build. DeepSeek at ~1-2% is effectively giving cache reads away, which sets the competitive floor.**
- ⚠️ **Attribution guard: these are LIST prices from public pricing pages as compiled by Bernstein on 2026-08-14, not realised blended rates. Enterprise contracts, batch discounts and committed-use pricing are not visible here, so do NOT convert this table into a revenue-mix estimate for any lab.**
- **Context in the note (Bernstein's own framing):** the tokenomics exhibit sits inside a *"memory-centric AI"* argument — that *"memory capacity and memory bandwidth is the main limitation to compute"* and that NAND is *"the most optimal solution for inference given inference emphasis on read (vs. write) and the explosion of context memory (i.e. KV-cache)."* Cross-ref [[SNDK]], [[MU]], [themes/hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md).

## 🆕 Recent signals (Aug 13) — 🔴 THE AGENTIC TURN GETS ITS FIRST HARD COMPUTE COEFFICIENT (**CPU:XPU GOES 1:4 → 1:2 → ~1:1**), AND MS RE-RUNS THE ROIC GRID UNDER OPEN WEIGHTS AND GETS THE **SAME** ANSWER

**(1) 🔴 THE COEFFICIENT THIS PAGE HAS BEEN MISSING FOR THE AGENTIC LEG — a token is no longer the unit of work; a TASK is, and a task fans out** (BofA Securities · Vivek Arya / Duksan Jang / Michael Mani / Liam Pharr, "Rise of the agents: raising CPU TAM (again) to $210bn", 2026-08-12, Price Objective Change). BofA raises **CY30E server CPU TAM to $210bn+ from ~$170bn** — *"nearly **5x** growth off the **~$35bn CY25** level, and still **2.5x** growth off a strong CY26E base"* (CY26E = **$61.4bn**), lifting the CY26-30 CAGR to **+36% from +30% prior**. The stated mechanism is the tokenmaxxing-relevant part: *"**agentic inference is pushing the CPU to GPU ratio from ~1:4 in the training era to ~1:2 for AI inference, and ultimately toward ~1:1 for agentic AI**, as CPUs become **the orchestration control plane**."* [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Vivek_on_CPU.html)
- **The hard version of that claim, and it is the most useful single row in the note because it is a MEASURED series rather than a slogan — BofA's AI CPU/XPU UNIT ratio: 19% / 20% / 33% / 59% (2022 / 2023 / 2024 / 2025) → 69% (26E) → 99% (27E) → 132% (28E) → 138% (29E) → 149% (30E).** Built off AI CPU unit TAM 1.0 / 1.3 / 3.7 / 9.7 / 16.3 / 28.5 / 38.4 / 45.3 / 53.1mn against XPU unit TAM 4.9 / 6.2 / 11.0 / 16.3 / 23.6 / 28.7 / 29.1 / 32.9 / 35.7mn. ⚠️ **Note what the series actually says and what the headline does not: the ratio does not stop at 1:1, it goes THROUGH it to ~1.5 CPUs per XPU by 2030 — and it gets there as much from XPU unit growth DECELERATING (+10.9% CAGR CY26-30) as from CPU units accelerating (+34.3%).** (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)
- **WHY IT MATTERS FOR TOKEN ECONOMICS, in BofA's own words — the fan-out: agentic AI *"does not simply ask a model one question and receive one answer. It **plans, retrieves context, calls tools, manages state, invokes APIs, interacts with databases, routes tasks across models, evaluates intermediate outputs, applies guardrails, and loops until a task is completed**"*, so that *"in agentic workflows, **a single user request can fan out into many CPU-bound subtasks**."*** ➜ **This page has modelled monetization per TOKEN. BofA's framing says the billable unit is drifting to the TASK, and that the incremental infrastructure a task consumes is disproportionately NON-accelerator. Two consequences worth carrying: (a) revenue-per-GW comparisons that assume an all-GPU rack understate the capital behind a served agentic token, and (b) "tokens/sec/GPU" — the throughput metric this page and MS both anchor to — is silent on the orchestration layer that agentic work actually bottlenecks on.** (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)
- **THE DECODE-PHASE DETAIL, which lands directly on this page's cost-per-token machinery: BofA splits inference into LOAD / PREFILL / DECODE and puts the CPU back into the last one — *"in decode, performance is heavily impacted by **KV cache reuse**"*, with the CPU responsible for *"**scheduling, token-by-token control flow, sampling, guardrails, logits processing, memory management, and KV cache routing**."*** Prefill (TTFT + KV-cache creation) stays *"heavily dependent on overall compute/large matrix multiplication abilities, i.e. GPU/accelerators and networking rather than CPUs"*, while load/initialization is *"largely dependent on overall disk (I/O) and CPU speed."* ➜ **Consistent with the SemiAnalysis serving-cost work already on this page (cache reads dominating real Claude Code / Codex spend, KV offload to DRAM): the marginal cost of a decoded token is increasingly a MEMORY-AND-SCHEDULING cost, not a FLOPs cost.** (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)
- **THE KEY DEBATE BofA POSES AND RESOLVES — SUBSTITUTION vs EXPANSION, resolved for expansion: *"we view CPUs are additive to overall system TAM, expanding the compute opportunity as agentic workloads **layer new CPU dense infrastructure alongside (not instead of) XPUs**"*; *"we expect CPUs to rise in importance **alongside accelerators, not instead of them**, with system bottlenecks broadening to **scheduling, memory management, data movement, retrieval, tool latency, networking, and concurrency**."*** The physical form is named: **CPU-only racks** for RAG pipelines, embedding services, vector databases, data prep, API/tool execution and small/mid-model inference — *"CPU racks expand the TAM by addressing **niche/tail workloads that had previously been too small or inefficient for expensive GPU compute racks** to address"* — with [[NVDA]]'s **Vera CPU-only rack (up to 256 Vera CPUs, liquid-cooled, launching alongside Vera Rubin in 2H26)** as the existence proof, and a full Vera Rubin pod pencilling to **1,088 CPUs vs 1,152 GPUs ≈ 1:1**. ⚠️ **Corroboration BofA leans on is macro, not workload-level: near-record GPU rental and memory spot prices as evidence of a broad compute shortage extending to CPUs. ⚠️ The PDF's text layer is badly scrambled by the watermark in exactly that paragraph, so it is paraphrased here, not quoted.** (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)
- **Where the CPU sits in the whole system, which is the number to carry: CPUs go to **~10% of a ~$2.2Tn CY30 data center systems TAM** *"vs just ~7% in the CY24-25 training era"* (chart basis 6.8% 2024 / 6.9% 2025 → 10.5% 2030E), split **~$30bn traditional/IaaS + ~$90bn AI cluster compute/head node + ~$90bn AI agentic standalone node**; AI CPUs reach **~86% of server CPU TAM by CY30**. Ratings in the note (verified against its own coverage-cluster table, because the mix is not uniform): **[[AMD]] BUY, PO $620 — top CPU pick; [[NVDA]] BUY, PO $350 — overall sector top pick; [[INTC]] BUY, PO $145; [[ARM]] NEUTRAL, PO $260; [[QCOM]] UNDERPERFORM, PO $180.** ⚠️ **ARM is the note's "fastest share gainer" and is still only NEUTRAL-rated — BofA's stated reason is that *"much of the value accrues to the licensees (i.e. NVDA)"* with ARM's own AGI CPU ramp *"still further out (CY28-30 for meaningful volume)"*. Do not read the share forecast as the rating.** The full TAM decomposition is filed on [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md). (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)

**(2) 🔴 MS RE-RUNS ITS GenAI ROIC GRID UNDER OPEN WEIGHTS — AND THE HYPERSCALER ANSWER IS UNCHANGED, WHICH IS THE FINDING** (Morgan Stanley · Brian Nowak / Julian Herrera / Gregory Gao / Nikhil Javeri / Kavya Narayanan, "Internet | North America — How Could Open-Weight Models Impact GenAI ROIC?", 2026-08-12, Industry View Attractive). Follows MS's "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC" (27 Jul 2026), which is this page's reference token-P&L grid. [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/INTERNET_20260812_2000.html)
- **THE DEFINITION, worth pinning because this page has used "open-weight" loosely: with open-weight models *"users take possession of the weights"* — **(1) free to download by any end user** *"rather than gated behind a subscription/closed API"*, **(2) able to be fine-tuned/re-trained on a user's proprietary data** *"vs. closed models served identically to everyone"*, and **(3) deployable where the user chooses (on-prem, in the cloud, or via API)**. Closed-weight models are *"only accessible via subscription/API, either direct from the model provider or through a hyperscaler's managed service, such as Bedrock, Vertex, Foundry."* MS's bottom line, verbatim: *"**open-weight models add distribution routes (renting GPUs or on-prem), tend to be lower cost, and enable more customization for enterprises/SMBs using them…all of which are important to driving adoption and tech diffusion across the economy**."*** (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)
- 🔴 **THE ROIC NUMBERS, AND THE DISCIPLINED READ IS THAT THEY DID NOT MOVE. Lab/model layer: token pricing base case **~$1.75 per million tokens**, throughput anchored to **2,000–3,500 tokens/sec/GPU** *"built off public inference benchmarks running workloads of **open-weighted DeepSeek-V4-Pro 1.6T on GB300 hardware**"* — and MS's conclusion: *"**Pricing is likely to go lower over time, but notably, these assumptions still generate ~20-60% ROIC on 1GW of owned GB300**."* Hyperscaler IaaS layer (Exhibit 3): 410,256 GB300 chips/GW, 3.6bn GPU-hours, 75% utilisation, $7.00-$10.00/hr → **revenue/GW $18.9 / 21.6 / 22.9 / 24.3 / 27.0bn**, total opex/GW **$7.6bn**, incremental EBIT margin **60-72%**, **ROIC 23% / 28% / 31% / 34% / 39%**.** ⚠️⚠️ **NOTHING NET-NEW IN THOSE TWO ROWS: the 23-39% IaaS band and the ~19-63% owned-infra band were both already logged on this page from the 07-27 note. What IS new is that MS re-ran them with open-weights explicitly in the frame — including an open-weight model as the throughput workload — and published the SAME bands 16 days later. On this page's own standards that is a re-affirmation, not a second datapoint, and it should not be counted as independent corroboration.** (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)
- **THE 3P-INFRA EXHIBIT, which is where the open-weights pressure actually shows up (Exhibit 2, NOPAT of a model-enabled API on 1GW of THIRD-PARTY infrastructure): capacity used for inference **50 / 60 / 65 / 70 / 80%**, tokens/sec/GPU **3,500 / 3,000 / 2,750 / 2,500 / 2,000**, token pricing **$1.00 / $1.50 / $1.75 / $2.00 / $2.50 per mn** → **revenue/GW $23 / $35 / $40 / $45 / $52bn**, against GPU rental **$7.00-$10.00/hr** → **cost of compute/GW $25 / $29 / $31 / $32 / $36bn**.** ➜ **Read the left-hand column: at $1.00/mn tokens on rented infrastructure the model layer generates $23bn of revenue against $25bn of compute cost — NEGATIVE. The open-weights price path is survivable for the labs only alongside throughput gains or owned infrastructure, which is precisely MS's conclusion.** Benchmark carried: **Muse Spark 1.1 blended pricing $1.58 (8K/1K I/O)** — the same anchor as the 07-27 note, unchanged. (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)
- 🔴 **THE PRICING-PRESSURE LEG AT THE MODEL LAYER, and the named event: *"Given their lower per-token price point, **open-weight models create pricing competition across the model layer**, and we have already seen multiple U.S. labs release lower-priced offerings (**[[META]]'s Muse Spark 1.2 released last week**)."* MS: lower token pricing *"translat[es] to **lower incremental unit economics for model providers**...putting a higher importance on **token throughput, adoption and building an ecosystem** (similar to other open builds like **Android**)"* — which is why *"new [[GOOG]] **Gemini Flash** models (its more efficient models) are **just as important as Gemini 4 being on the frontier** later this year, in our view."*** ➜ **The Android analogy is the sharpest strategic statement here: MS is arguing the model layer is commoditising toward an ECOSYSTEM game, in which the frontier model is a marketing asset and the efficient model is the P&L.** (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)
- 🔴 **THE THROUGHPUT-IMPROVEMENT TAXONOMY — four levers, and the fourth is a RETURNS-TO-SCALE argument this page has not carried: (1) *"better hardware (more memory capacity at chip level and rack level) and faster interconnects"*, (2) *"increasing model sparsity within MoE models (active parameters/total parameters)"*, (3) *"software improvements from **request batching**"*, and (4) *"we believe hyperscalers drive their own throughput gains… from both **proprietary hardware (custom silicon via TPUs/Trainium)** and **software (hyperscalers have the deepest enterprise footprints and thus the highest volume of requests to batch)**."*** ➜ **Lever (4) says token throughput is partly a function of REQUEST VOLUME, not only of silicon — batching efficiency scales with the size of the request pool. If that holds, cost-per-token is structurally lower at the largest clouds regardless of chip choice, and the smaller neoclouds and single-tenant labs cannot close it by buying the same hardware. That is the most load-bearing new claim in the note and it is asserted, not quantified — no batching-efficiency curve is shown.** Cross-filed [custom-asic-tpu](custom-asic-tpu.md). (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)
- **THE FOUR REASONS HYPERSCALER UNIT ECONOMICS SURVIVE CHEAPER MODELS, compressed: (1) *"**Compute is the scarce resource**, and enterprises rely on hyperscalers to provide compute necessary for open-weight inference workloads"*; (2) they will monetize to maximise ROIC and cheaper models mean *"**substantially more volume at lower per token or GW unit economics** — **EBIT dollar growth matters more than percentage margins here**"*; (3) continued focus on throughput; (4) all GenAI workloads *"drive **attach of other services** and incremental profitability."* The monetization paths on open models: serverless API token revenue, accelerator rental, **RAG (driving storage and database revenue)** and security/governance — with the concession that *"**API token revenue on open models may be a loss leader (like milk in a grocery store)**, [but] open models have the potential to generate **more core cloud infrastructure attach per workload**."* Bottom line: *"investors should remain focused on the hyperscaler (or owner of compute) that has the **lowest cost to serve** (through custom silicon, unique 1P models/offerings and/or other software/hardware advances). **We remain OW [[AMZN]] and [[GOOG]]** for these reasons."* PTs in the note: **META $775 OW (Top Pick), AMZN $335 OW, GOOGL $400 OW.** ⚠️ **UBER, RDDT and BKNG appear in this note only in disclosure boilerplate — not routed.** (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-08-12)

**(3) A FIRST-PARTY TOKEN-VOLUME AND MODEL-CHOICE DATAPOINT FROM [[MSFT]] IR** (Microsoft IR call — Mary, 2026-08-13). *"[The AI platform / Foundry] has **over 350 customers on track to process more than a trillion tokens this year**."* Model breadth: *"We offered the largest selections of models… in the cloud, **over 11,000**"*, plus customer fine-tuning — *"take that data that's unique to you, fine tune one of our models, **you create your own model**."* The stated rationale for a multi-model world is a direct statement of the token-cost discipline this page tracks: *"customers will always **optimize cost for outcome**. They're going to want to ensure that they're optimizing the right model for the right task so they can be **the most efficient in token spend**"* — plus business continuity/resiliency. ➜ **Corroborates MS's multi-model conclusion from the buyer's side, and note the shape of the disclosure: 350+ customers EACH on track for >1tn tokens this year is a COUNT OF LARGE ACCOUNTS, not a platform total — it sets a floor (>350tn tokens) and nothing else. Do not treat it as an Azure token-volume disclosure.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_08_13_ir_msft_post_2q.html)
- **The margin claim, quoted exactly because the paraphrase in circulation is stronger than what was said: *"we don't have a reason to believe now that Azure revenue Azure margins in the long term look **materially different with AI in the picture than without**"* (verbatim, transcription artefact included). ⚠️ **That is a NEGATIVE construction with NO timeframe attached — "we don't have a reason to believe" — not an affirmative margin guide, and the widely-relayed rendering as "materially similar" overstates it.** The levers named are the tokenmaxxing-relevant part: effective pricing (*"every new deal… we're thinking about discounting"*; *"every new SKU that comes on is priced with the current pricing environment in mind"*) plus a compounding efficiency stack — *"you can almost think of it as **the capacity today is as inefficient as it will ever be**, because… **you'll see silicon get more efficient, you'll see models get more efficient, you'll see the routing mechanism for models get more efficient, software, things like token throughput**… Even operationally, like **how fast you can plug in a GPU**."* ➜ **Management is underwriting long-run margin on a THROUGHPUT-PER-GW improvement curve — the same variable MS makes the hinge of model-layer ROIC. It is the cleanest first-party statement on the wiki that revenue/GW is expected to rise from efficiency rather than from price.** (Microsoft IR call — Mary, 2026-08-13)

## 🆕 Recent signals (Aug 12) — 🔴 THE BEST AVAILABLE COUNTER TO THE TOKENMAXXING-REDUCTION FEAR IS A REAL ENTERPRISE CASE STUDY — AND IT CUTS *SPEND* ~62% WITH VOLUME HELD FLAT, WHICH IS TWO-SIDED

**(1) 🔴 THE RIPPLING CASE STUDY — the cleanest datapoint yet on whether open-weights adoption destroys token demand or just token price** (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd / Sabrina Hao / Brian Nowak, "The Model Race Gets More Complicated: Chinese Model Pricing & Open-Weights Adoption", 2026-08-12). *"**Rippling… was able to use open-weights models (GLM 5.2) and a custom model routing layer to reduce their token spend from 40% of R&D headcount to 15% without cutting token usage (held stable at ~600B tokens/month)**."* MS: *"examples like this should further dispel fears of a reduction in tokenmaxxing, as it shows enterprises can **use open-weights models to 'do more with less'**."*
- ⚠️ **The read is two-sided and this page should carry both halves: volume held FLAT while spend fell ~62%. That is a revenue-per-token DEFLATION datapoint as much as it is a volume-defence datapoint — good for adoption and for Jevons, not obviously good for anyone monetising tokens.** The bull case on this page has generally been "volume grows faster than price falls." Rippling is a case where **price fell and volume did not grow at all**.
- **Corroborating enterprise commentary in the same note, all from earnings calls:** **Pinterest** runs open-source models post-trained on its own data plus a router layer, achieving *"cost per transaction at **less than 8% of the cost** of comparable closed proprietary models"* · **Revolve** — *"open source and some of these lower-cost models is going to be a huge part of the mix. **It's already a huge part of our mix**"* · **Duolingo** — *"it is in our best interest as a company to use open weight models as much as possible, so **if I had a magic wand, I would try to move everything to an open weight model**"* · **[[SPOT]]** lets users *"switch models mid-task and route every job to the best available performance."* [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/GLOBAL_20260812_0401.html)

**(2) 🔴 TOKEN PRICING IS NOW FALLING FROM ONE END AND *RISING* FROM THE OTHER — the Chinese leg has inverted.** MS cites **Silicon Data's LLM Token Price Index**: overall token prices are declining on (a) mix shifting toward open-weights and (b) closed-weight price cuts. **But: *"prices for Chinese models – which were historically viewed as cheap and small – are RISING, while frontier model prices are DECLINING."***
- **Mechanics, three of them.** **DeepSeek is raising API prices directly** (magnitude undisclosed, *"reported to be 'significant'"*); MS's own analysis says a **2x increase on V4-Flash from $0.14 per 1M input / $0.28 per 1M output** would put a **200MW data centre running V4-Flash in line with Kimi K3 and other frontier models on margin**. ⚠️ **Note the carve-out, which matters for the whole open-weights argument: the increase applies only to API consumption, NOT to customers who download and fine-tune the model.** Plus **commercial licensing from Moonshot and Alibaba**, requiring enterprises earning **$20M+** off these models to negotiate separate terms — **Reuters reports Moonshot can take *"up to 30% revenue share"***, which MS says *"would largely apply to hyperscalers serving these models rather than individual enterprise customers."* ➜ **A departure from historically permissive open-weight licences, and the first sign that "open" is being monetised rather than given away.**
- **Chinese LLM APIs remain at roughly 15-20% of US peer pricing**, but MS expects the gap to narrow as US providers keep cutting low/mid-tier prices. ⚠️ **The risk MS flags is the one this page should track: higher Chinese-lab profitability could fund a reinvestment flywheel that closes the CAPABILITY gap faster.**

**(3) MS's own conclusion: *"evidence increasingly points to a MULTI-MODEL WORLD, with open-weights driving tangible enterprise ROI,"* with *"the US/China model price differential"* named as the key swing factor to watch.** ➜ **Cross-filed to [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md), where the same note's "3 States of the World" grid makes the capex-composition argument.**

## 🆕 Recent signals (Aug 11) — JEVONS, MEASURED LIVE: the house ran the [[OPENAI]] GPT-5.6 Luna repricing as an event study on OpenRouter — the demand response is ~UNIT-ELASTIC (ε ≈ −1.0), BELOW the 1.2–1.8 coefficient logged 08-06

**🔴🔴 ★ THE PAGE'S FIRST MEASURED JEVONS EXPERIMENT — and the canonical worked example of the paradox for this wiki** (house event study on OpenRouter public data, fetched 2026-08-11; [interactive exhibit](../_data/openrouter/luna_jevons.artifact.html), raw series cached in `_data/openrouter/raw/2026-08-11/`; independently re-audited before publication — the adversarial recompute confirmed 40/42 figures and corrected the draft headline, see basis bullet). [[OPENAI]] repriced GPT-5.6 Luna twice in four days: a **50% first-party discount on its OpenRouter endpoint (Jul 27 17:01 UTC)**, then the **80% list cut (Jul 30 17:11 UTC** — @sama/@gdb; same day DeepSeek shipped V4-Flash-0731) — effective venue price **$1/$6 → $0.10/$0.60 per M tokens, −91% listed / −93% realized** (cache-hit mix rose 76.8%→86.0%). Clean 7-day windows either side, mixed step-days excluded:

- **Volume: ×13.5** — 49.6bn → 671.5bn tokens/day (requests ×6.9, tokens/request ×2.0); Luna went from 0.5% to **7.4% of ALL OpenRouter tokens (#4 model on the venue)**. The classic Jevons half of the story is intact: consumption exploded far faster than price fell per unit of *served* compute.
- **Arc elasticity ≈ −1.0 — UNIT-ELASTIC, i.e. REVENUE-NEUTRAL, not revenue-accretive**: −0.99 on OpenRouter's own effective-paid price series, −0.83…−1.13 across every basis and pre-trend counterfactual. Sol ($5/$30, never cut — the control tier) grew only ×1.15 over the identical window, so this is not market drift. Terra (−62% effective) did ×4.49 → ε −1.55 (cache-off basis only).
- **⚠️ THE SPEND SIGN DEPENDS ON THE PRICE BASIS, AND THE NAIVE BASIS FLIPS IT:** tokens × listed price (cache-off) says implied Luna spend **+23.6%** — but the cache-hit shift (reads bill at 1/10th) reverses it: **−13.8%** cache-on flat-tier, **−2.0% (≈ flat)** on OR's effective series, which embeds both caching and the >272K-context 2× tier. **Standing correction for this page: the implied-$ ceiling (~2-3× realized) is NOT sign-preserving when cache mix moves — no tokens×list-price Jevons claim should be quoted without the cache-adjusted companion.**
- **🔴 VENDOR-LEVEL THERE WAS NO JEVONS AT ALL ON THIS VENUE: OpenAI's TOTAL implied take on OpenRouter FELL −14.8%** ($8.82M → $7.52M/wk; tokens ×2.21) as the premium book collapsed into Luna — gpt-5.5 ×0.50, gpt-5.4 ×0.49, gpt-5 ×0.23, o4-mini ×0.21, sol-pro ×0.46. Luna's implied gain (+$102k/wk) offset **1/14th** of the ex-Luna decline (−$1,402k/wk).
- **⚠️ Composition unresolved — a large share looks like RE-ROUTING, not net-new demand:** 67% of the entire level shift completed within 48h of the cut (77bn → 477bn/day by Jul 31 — induced demand needs build/adopt cycles; a 2-day step is what promo-chasing re-routing looks like); one app (Hermes Agent, OpenRouter's #1) grew w/w by the equivalent of **82% of Luna's entire absolute gain** (concentration, not attribution — apps aren't broken out by model). OpenRouter is the price-sensitive multi-model routing venue (~70tn tok/wk), so **market-wide elasticity is plausibly LOWER than measured here**.
- **The discount and the cut were two different events:** in the 48h when only the −50% discount was live, volume moved just ×1.48 → short-run ε ≈ −0.5 and implied spend FELL ~31-35% on both bases; the explosion came only with the list cut. Adoption lags price — the POST window itself may still understate the steady-state response (tokens were still making highs on Aug 10).
- **Status of the 08-06 Exponential View coefficient (item 1 of the Aug 6-9 block below): THE SECOND DATAPOINT HAS ARRIVED, IT IS MEASURED, AND IT LANDS BELOW THE BAND.** Direction corroborated — token demand is elastic-ish and a deep cut is ~self-funding — but the 1.2–1.8 range is not: Luna reads ~1.0 on every basis (only Terra's cache-off −1.55 reaches the band). Per this page's own rule the coefficient stays OUT of [_meta/assumptions.md](../_meta/assumptions.md); anyone using one should use **~1.0-1.2 with the venue caveat**, not 1.2-1.8.
- **Watch item:** the 50% OpenRouter discount is flagged as a *discount*, not a list change — **if it lapses, Luna's effective price doubles overnight = a free reversal experiment on token stickiness** (`or_fetch.py` now snapshots Luna/Terra endpoint pricing weekly). Competitive frame: the cut landed the day DeepSeek shipped V4-Flash-0731 ($0.09/$0.18), which itself ×2.4'd to 15.1tn tok/wk (#1 on the venue, 21.4% share) — the whole cheap tier repriced at once.

## Recent signals (Aug 6-9) — the demand curve gets a NUMBER, the premium "fast tier" gets repriced by open-source software, and the same GPU is worth 2-8x more serving an open model than leasing it to a lab

**1. 🔴🔴 ★ THE ELASTICITY, QUANTIFIED — this page has argued price-cuts-drive-volume since June without a coefficient. It now has one: "every 10% REDUCTION IN TOKEN PRICE DRIVES A 12% TO 18% INCREASE IN TOKEN CONSUMPTION"** (Exponential View, cited by 13D Research & Strategy, "What I Learned This Week", excerpt 2026-08-06). ⚠️ **That is elasticity of 1.2–1.8 — ELASTIC, so revenue RISES on a price cut, which is the arithmetic backbone of every "cut price, expand market" argument on this wiki. Basis unstated (workload mix, time window, whether it is measured on marketplace routing or first-party APIs), so treat it as a directional coefficient from a third party, NOT as a canonical assumption. It belongs in [_meta/assumptions.md](../_meta/assumptions.md) only if a second independent source corroborates it.**

**2. 🔴 THE SAME GPU-HOUR IS WORTH 2–8x MORE SERVING AN OPEN MODEL THAN LEASING IT TO A FRONTIER LAB — and the lease numbers come from a filing.** Exponential View's estimate (via 13D, 2026-08-06): "a GPU OWNER OR CLOUD PROVIDER CAN EARN **$60 PER GPU-HOUR IN REVENUE** by HOSTING KIMI K3 for customer use in a STANDARD CHATBOT WORKLOAD, or **$23 PER GPU-HOUR FOR A COMPLEX AGENTIC WORKLOAD**, versus **$7.80 TO $11.50 PER GPU-HOUR** by LEASING THAT GPU CAPACITY TO A FRONTIER LAB." **The two lease points are named and sourced to primary documents: "[[SPCX]] lease to [[ANTHROPIC]] $7.80" and "[[SPCX]] lease to [[GOOG]] $11.50" (source line: "SpaceX S-1 filing & Google agreement; Kimi K3 API pricing; EV analysis").** ⚠️ **READ THE BASES BEFORE USING THIS. (a) The $60/$23 legs are MODELLED REVENUE at published Kimi K3 API pricing at unstated utilisation — they are a gross-revenue-per-hour ceiling, not a margin, and they carry all the serving risk the lease does not. (b) The lease legs are CONTRACTED revenue with the counterparty carrying utilisation risk. Comparing them directly is comparing a merchant business to a landlord business. (c) Do NOT net this against SemiAnalysis's SPCX pricing of "$30–50M/MW/year" (2026-08-07) — different units and different bases ($/GPU-hour vs $/MW/year); the wiki's standing rule against mixing per-chip and per-watt bases applies. Both marks are logged on [[SPCX]] with their own attribution.** ✅ **What it IS good for: it prices the strategic question the neocloud pages keep circling — whether compute owners stay landlords or move up into serving. On these numbers the incentive to move up is large, which is a demand argument for accelerators and a margin argument against the labs.**

**3. 🔴🔴 ★ THE PREMIUM FAST TIER JUST GOT REPRICED BY OPEN-SOURCE SOFTWARE, AND THE COMPARISON NAMES A LAB'S PRODUCT.** SemiAnalysis, "Ultra-High Interactivity on NVIDIA GPUs? - TileRT InferenceX" (published 2026-08-09): a persistent-kernel decode engine gets an **eight-GPU B200 node to 340 tokens/s/user at 8k/1k for $13.56 PER MILLION OUTPUT TOKENS**, against **$13.4/M at 176 tok/s/user** for the best conventional FP4 point (GB200 FP4+MTP at concurrency 5, $1.86/GB200-hour) — **"only 1% MORE PER TOKEN WHILE DELIVERING 1.9× THE INTERACTIVITY"** — and against **~$35/M** for the fastest conventional FP8 endpoint (GB300, 108 tok/s/user), i.e. **"61% CHEAPER PER OUTPUT TOKEN WHILE DELIVERING 3.1× THE INTERACTIVITY."** 🔴 **The benchmark they choose is the tell: "this is MUCH MORE VALUE THAN CLAUDE CODE FAST MODE, UP TO 2.5× THE INTERACTIVITY BUT 2× THE PRICE PER TOKEN."** ⚠️ **The premise underneath is the page's own thesis, stated by a third party: "PREMIUM-PRICED 'FAST MODES' ARE PROVING THAT USERS WILL PAY MORE FOR LOWER LATENCY AND FASTER TOKENS, POTENTIALLY YIELDING HIGHER GROSS MARGINS. Frontier AI labs such as [[OPENAI]] are therefore EVALUATING PURPOSE-BUILT INFERENCE SYSTEMS, INCLUDING [[CEREBRAS]] AND NVIDIA GROQ LPUs." So latency is confirmed as a PRICEABLE product attribute — and simultaneously shown to be cheaper to supply than the current fast-tier pricing implies. Those two facts point in opposite directions for lab gross margin and the same direction for token volume.** ⚠️ **Basis limits: batch size 1 (one in-flight request per decode node in v0.1.5), a two-model catalog (GLM-5/5.1, DeepSeek-V3.2), and lower aggregate throughput than a concurrency-12 GB300 point (160.4 vs ~240 tok/s/GPU). This is a per-user-speed result, not a fleet-economics result. Full detail on [[NVDA]] and [[CEREBRAS]].**

**4. The agentic workload shape gets a public specification, which matters for every cost-per-task number on this page.** SemiAnalysis's AgentX benchmark "replays REAL CLAUDE CODE AND CODEX TRACES with long-context, multi-turn requests, realistic subagent activity, and dynamic tool-use delays. Its **MEDIAN INPUT LENGTH IS 140K TOKENS**, while the **theoretical median CACHE-HIT RATE ROOFLINE REACHES 99.2%**" (2026-08-09). ✅ **A 140k median input with a 99%+ cache-hit roofline is why cache-read pricing dominates agentic economics and why headline per-token prices understate the real cost curve — the mechanism behind the input/cache-read/cache-write/output blending this wiki has been carrying qualitatively.**

**5. And the price action that frames all of the above: [[OPENAI]] cut GPT-5.6 Luna-Max's price by 80% ON THE SAME DAY DeepSeek released V4 Flash, which "remains 60% CHEAPER even after OpenAI's price cut"** (13D, 2026-08-06). **The CFRA cost ladder from the same note: a task costing $1.80 on [[ANTHROPIC]]'s Claude Opus 4.8 runs at ~$0.95 on Kimi K3 and $0.04 on DeepSeek's V4 Pro.** ⚠️ **Third-party arithmetic on undisclosed workload assumptions; a ~45x top-to-bottom spread is doing a lot of work. Attributed, not adopted. See [china-export](china-export.md) for the adoption series that goes with it (Chinese models 48% of OpenRouter traffic in late June, from 20% a year earlier).**

## Recent signals (Aug 10, PM) — 🔴 THE PHASE CHANGE THIS PAGE IS NAMED AFTER IS REPORTED AS **DONE**: ~15 ENTERPRISE IT EXECS SAY THEY HAVE MOVED FROM TOKEN-MAXXING TO **TOKEN OPTIMIZATION** — AND SPEND HAS NOT FALLEN

**🔴 UBS ran the check that most directly tests this page's core question (UBS AI Research · Karl Keirstead · Jack Fyda, "Latest Enterprise AI Checks", 2026-08-10).** Basis: the **Ai4 conference in Las Vegas**, ~**15 enterprise IT executives** plus industry partners.
- **The finding, verbatim:** *"It is very clear from our checks that **many enterprises have pivoted from an initial phase of AI TOKEN-MAXXING to a new phase marked by TOKEN OPTIMIZATION**, moving forward with AI initiatives but with **efficiency and ROI more firmly in mind**."*
- **🔴 AND THE CRUCIAL SECOND HALF, WHICH IS THE BULL DATAPOINT:** *"The concern that we and many investors have expressed is whether this greater focus on ROI and efficient AI spend may serve to **weigh on AI spending growth, impacting the frontier labs [[ANTHROPIC]] and [[OPENAI]] and cascading into the broader AI trade**. **But at least among these enterprises, this has NOT yet translated into any pullback on AI spend, which remains strong**."* ⚠️ **This is the cleanest available evidence that optimization ≠ contraction — the Jevons argument, observed rather than asserted.** It is also, however, **~15 firms self-reporting**, not spend data — treat as directional.
- **⚠️ THE OPEN-SOURCE ROUTING THESIS IS PARTIALLY CONTRADICTED, AND THE NUANCE MATTERS.** *"One of the dominant methods to lower the cost of AI tasks is to **route tokens to open-source models**, be it **Nvidia Nemotron** or a host of very performant **Chinese AI models**. **In this round of checks — which were mostly large and non-tech enterprises — the appetite to use Chinese models was LOW**, evidence of **how early-stage we are in the model-diversification trend**."* ⚠️ **So: routing-to-open-source is the acknowledged cost lever, but the Chinese-model leg specifically is not yet adopted at large non-tech enterprises.** That bounds the near-term margin-transfer argument this page has been building since Aug 2-4 — the mechanism is real, the adoption is not there yet in this cohort. **Enterprises show "a preference for FRONTIER MODELS."**
- **Build-vs-buy is unresolved and the incumbents are losing it so far:** *"in these checks we **STILL repeatedly hear many F500 enterprises (not all) express a preference to just BUILD THEIR OWN AI products**, a trend that **weighs on the growth of AI products from SaaS/apps firms."* ⚠️ Direct read-through against the applications layer and in favour of the infrastructure layer — same direction as the corroborating adoption data [[SPCX]]'s analyst cites the same day (Ramp AI Index: **median monthly AI spend per employee ~$11 in June, +167% y/y; top decile $516; top 1% >$4,880**; **~55% of US businesses now pay for AI**, i.e. *"close to half still pay nothing"*; Jellyfish: **the median company still ships <30% of its code with AI help**, up from ~zero a year earlier — Morgan Stanley / Adam Jonas, 2026-08-10).
- **Where the tokens actually run:** *"We do **not** hear many anecdotes of enterprises scaling up their AI hardware and software stacks **on-premise** — their AI solutions are **almost always built and/or hosted on AWS, Azure and/or Google Cloud**. **AI is primarily happening in the cloud, not on-premise**."* Named beneficiaries in the surrounding stack: **Databricks (cited numerous times), [[PLTR]] and Snowflake**; at the database layer *"Redis and Postgres"*, which UBS reads as **MongoDB facing material competition for AI workloads**. Public apps firms cited were few (**Atlassian, [[CRWD]], Salesforce**) against *"the number of smaller privately-held vendors flagged."* Cross-ref [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md).

So they set up a router, and that actually CUTS their AI spend, but it may actually INCREASE the amount of tokens they are generating, just by shifting them to cheaper open-source tokens. And that's just more compute… the GPU compute-hours probably are going up."** The product version: Fireworks' **Nexus** — "if you're using Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, Grok Build, it is literally THREE LINES OF CODE… Fireworks ingests your data, they RL a model, and a router sends the query" first to your model, with a frontier model checking — **"in a lot of cases slightly better outcomes at HALF THE COST. But that half-the-cost… the savings are coming from the margin on the tokens — the tokens cost the same amount of compute to produce."** Frontier models retain ~**30-60% of token consumption** (planning/orchestration/verification — Cursor-style frontier-plans-cheap-executes patterns cited at ~15x efficiency, cf. [[SPCX]]); Cursor and Harvey ("before it was acquired") leaned in hardest — **"all of a sudden you're not a wrapper, you're way more defensible."**
- **④ Open source as "DARK MATTER to the public markets": the US open-source inference clouds — Fireworks, Baseten, Modal, Together — "have accelerated massively because of GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3" and are "growing almost as fast as the frontier labs in the early days but BURNING VERY LITTLE CASH… from a rule-of-40 perspective these are crazy numbers."** Plus [[NVDA]] "bringing Nemotron steadily closer to the frontier." The public market can't see any of it — which is his explanation for why the tape keeps mispricing token-demand news.
- **⑤ The spend-side sizing, all informal but directionally consistent with this page's TAM rows: token spend as % of total comp at AI-native startups "gets really high — 20%, 25%" (O'Shaughnessy, from his portfolio), one company cited at ~30%, Baker has "actually heard of 50." Against "$25 TRILLION in knowledge work," a 20% token-share of comp = ~$5T — which "either comes out of labor substitution or faster economic growth, and we really really want it to come from growth."** Labor substitution is running through NOT-HIRING rather than layoffs (GP$/FTE "vertical" per a16z/Iconiq work on AI natives; founder-controlled companies conspicuously NOT doing mass layoffs). Adoption gradient: AI natives all-in → coastal early adopters routing → "companies on the East Coast who have barely adopted AI" → Europe regulating first — "differential waves of adoption all happening at the same time." And the demand overhang he can't shake: **~250-500k people using agentic AI today, IN AN ACUTE COMPUTE SHORTAGE — "what happens when we go to 100 million, to 500 million?"**
- **⑥ Value distribution: cheap open-source models may INFLATE frontier pricing power at the top — "if you have 120-IQ open-source models that are really cheap to run, doesn't that make a 160-IQ model that can ORCHESTRATE them more valuable?… Frontier tokens may continue to capture the overwhelming majority of economic value — but not all of it the way they have been — and open-source tokens might be the MAJORITY OF TOKENS PROCESSED. And that's great for infrastructure demand."** The lab-maximalist counter-scenario is logged, not adopted: post-RSI frontier models distill down to dominate every intelligence-per-dollar point and "there's no place for open source" — "we shouldn't dismiss anything… but that doesn't seem that likely," because AI natives now hold domain-specific proprietary data and independence from frontier terms-of-service has its own value. Dwarkesh's decomposition of the private-side acceleration, relayed: **compute margins UP × compute volume UP × inference margins UP** (his $250k/yr H100 = ~15x spot as the tail marker).

A revenue-run-rate-independent re-rating is precisely the mechanism that would break the "backlog is $2.3tn so capex is safe" argument without any customer cancelling anything.**

They can move much faster, especially if they're primarily deploying [[NVDA]]'s platform and working directly with NVDA to optimize the entire stack"** (@The_AI_Investor, 2026-08-01). **Relevant here because the neocloud-vs-hyperscaler cost-per-token gap is one of the arbitrage channels through which efficiency gains would show up in prices. See [[CRWV]], [[NBIS]].**

**★ ② [[OPENAI]]'s REPOSITIONING IS BEING READ AS AN ANSWER TO THIS THEME, NOT A COINCIDENCE.** **"Most interesting 2026 VIBE SHIFT is OPENAI ACTUALLY LOOKING TO BE THE OPEN PLATFORM. Note the marked difference: INTELLIGENCE ON TAP AS A UTILITY vs signaling it is optimal to INTEGRATE ALL THE WAY UP FULL STACK"** (@garrytan, 2026-08-01, 337 likes). ⚠️ **Set that beside the two facts already on this wiki from 08-01: OpenAI CUT PRICES ON SMALLER MODELS "in response to token maxing," and the containment breach widened from Hugging Face to five platforms. Three data points now point the same way — the frontier lab with the most to lose from commoditisation is behaving as though it is happening, and is choosing to compete as the utility layer rather than defend a full-stack premium. If that reading is right, the margin structure the market has been modelling for OpenAI (application-software gross margins) is the wrong one, and the price cuts are STRATEGY rather than distress.**
- **The offsetting demand fact, and it is genuinely offsetting: OpenAI's "JULY ARR ALREADY EXCEEDING THE ENTIRETY OF 2Q REVENUE, suggesting the company MAY BE GETTING BACK ON TRACK," with Jefferies arguing "renewed momentum should support demand across GPUs, custom accelerators, networking, optics, AND MEMORY"** (Jefferies · Blayne Curtis, 2026-08-02). ⚠️ **Token efficiency and token volume are not the same variable. This theme's core question is whether efficiency gains outrun volume growth — and this week produced evidence of BOTH accelerating at once, which is exactly the ambiguous state that has kept the debate unresolved. The 22V claim that "[[ANTHROPIC]] and OpenAI [are] PACING TOWARD $175B IN COMBINED REVENUE" (Visser, 2026-08-02) is NOT ADOPTED — no basis, no period, no split — but it is the highest combined figure published to date.**
- **Product evidence that the agentic layer is shipping (which raises tokens per task even as cost per token falls): OpenAI's president spent the weekend demonstrating "CHATGPT WORK" — a cloud browser that "lets you easily MONITOR what your AI is up to and also INTERVENE with the live application if needed," plus recurring-task automation (@gdb, 2026-08-01/02). And the capability marker of the window, on the [[ANTHROPIC]] side: Karpathy gave Opus 5 the first paragraph of Lord of the Rings, "A 1M TOKEN BUDGET (~$10)," and it "WENT OFF FOR ~2 HOURS AND WROTE 5500 LINES OF CODE" procedurally rendering the story (@karpathy, 2026-08-02, 16k likes).** ⚠️ **That last one is the cleanest single illustration of the theme's central tension: ~$10 and 1M tokens for a task that was not possible at any price 18 months ago. Cost per token collapsing, tokens per task exploding, and the product frontier moving to whatever the new budget affords. Anyone forecasting inference demand off cost-per-token alone is modelling one of three moving terms.**
- **Adjacent commoditisation datapoint from the model-supply side: "SILICON VALLEY STARTUPS ARE BUILDING OPEN MODELS TO COUNTER CHINA'S SURGE, with some operating on SHOESTRING BUDGETS owing to LIMITED INTEREST FROM VCs"** (WSJ, 2026-08-02). ⚠️ **Cuts against Path3: if Western open-weight efforts are capital-starved, the open-model pressure on pricing comes disproportionately from Chinese labs (Kimi K3, DeepSeek lineage) — which makes it a geopolitical variable as much as an economic one. See [themes/china-export](china-export.md).**

## What it is / why it matters
"Tokenmaxxing" was the 2025 reflex of enterprises (and their employees) maxing out token consumption to look AI-forward; the 2026 sequel is the pullback — firms now demanding **control** over token spend, routing cheaper tasks to cheaper models, and testing open-source/DeepSeek substitutes. The buy-side question this resolves: **does economic value keep accruing to frontier models (Anthropic/OpenAI pricing power, and the GPU capex that underwrites it), or does model substitution and cost discipline cap the monetization curve?** The on-disk evidence (Jun 2026) is two-sided: spend is still climbing fast and the frontier still captures ~90% of revenue, but a cost-control backlash is real on the margin and is starting to move enterprise behavior, vendor pricing (MSFT Copilot → usage), and the read-through to hyperscaler demand.

We replay an hour of these traces as they reach a steady state. There is a MEDIAN OF 142K INPUT TOKENS and a MEDIAN OF 444 OUTPUT TOKENS PER TURN with a MEDIAN OF 65 TURNS PER SESSION. The SHORT OUTPUT TOKENS PER TURN IS TYPICAL FOR WORKLOADS ON AGENTIC HARNESSES, WHERE THE AGENT CALLS TOOLS FREQUENTLY, EVEN EDITS ARE TOOL USES. This benchmark is A BIG STEP UP FROM OUR PREVIOUS 8K1K/1K1K BENCHMARK, as it TRULY REFLECTS REAL-WORLD AGENTIC USE CASES… It can reflect KV cache behavior, including PREFIX CACHE AND KV OFFLOADING TO DRAM."** ⚠️ **The methodological point is an investment point: the industry's serving-cost benchmarks were built on synthetic short-context prompts, and the real agentic shape is ~142k in / ~444 out per turn over ~65 turns. That is a 300:1-plus context-to-output profile, which relocates the binding constraint from decode throughput to CONTEXT/CACHE ECONOMICS — and it is why the authors' own bill looks the way it does: "from SemiAnalysis Claude Code and Codex usage, COSTS FROM CACHE READ MAKE UP MOST OF OUR SPENDING." A page tracking whether enterprises can control token spend should note that the biggest line item in a heavy agentic user's bill is cache reads, not generation.**
  - **THE SUPPLY-SIDE DETAIL THAT MAKES THE ABOVE REPEATABLE RATHER THAN A ONE-OFF: "for Kimi K3, DAY 0 BRINGUP WAS EASIER THAN DSv4 due to better documentation and preparation ahead of weights release. Appropriate images and a SPECULATIVE DECODER MODEL were released at the same time as the weights," and "BOTH NVIDIA AND AMD HAD DAY 0 RECIPES ON vLLM, boasting DRAM OFFLOAD AND DSPARK SPECULATIVE DECODING."** ⚠️ **Open-weight releases are now shipping with the serving stack attached, and both accelerator vendors ship day-0 recipes. The commoditisation channel this page has been arguing about is therefore getting FASTER, not just cheaper: the lag between a frontier-class open model existing and being cheap to self-serve at scale is collapsing toward zero. Also note where the day-0 gap now sits — the model "doesn't fit on a single B200 node. We had to use PP to get it working. DSpark also didn't work with PP" — i.e. the memory footprint, not the software, is what excludes the prior generation.**
  - **THE ARCHITECTURAL DRIVER BEHIND THE FALLING COST, worth carrying because it says the trend is structural rather than a pricing decision: K3's serving economics come from COMPRESSED KV MEMORY (hybrid KDA/MLA linear attention), ATTENTION ACROSS DEPTH and LATENT EXPERT ROUTING — architecture choices that cut the KV footprint per token. The same communication-to-computation-ratio logic is pushing the OPPOSITE parameter up: SemiAnalysis derives T_comm/T_comp = (P·F)/(6·m·B)·(1−1/E) and reads it as the reason for "an increase in EXPERT INTERMEDIATE DIMENSION TO 3,072 in not just Kimi K2 to K3, but ALL RECENT OPEN WEIGHT MODELS, including DEEPSEEK V4 PRO, MINIMAX M3, MIMO V2.5 PRO, and INKLING. As hardware improves and expert weight precision reduces to save memory capacity, the compute throughput increases, so one way of reducing the ratio is by increasing the expert intermediate dimension."** ⚠️ **Read as a demand statement rather than a research note: rising accelerator throughput and lower expert-weight precision let labs hide more of the MoE all-to-all behind compute, and they are all spending that headroom the same way — wider experts. Convergent design choices across five independent labs mean the efficiency gains are a moving industry baseline, not a proprietary edge, which is the supply-side half of the commoditisation case in the Aug-2 item below.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Semianalysis_on_K3.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-30) — 🔴 THE TWO HALVES OF THE TOKENMAXXING DEBATE LANDED IN THE SAME WEEK AND THEY POINT OPPOSITE WAYS. Morgan Stanley argues on data that the token-budget fear is invalid; Microsoft's own IR says the behaviour shift is already happening and surprised them with its speed. Both are on this page now — do not collapse them.**
  - **SIDE A — MORGAN STANLEY SAYS THE CONCERN IS NOT VALID, AND PUTS THREE NUMBERS UNDER IT (Morgan Stanley "Playing the AI Infrastructure Dip", Byrd/Weaver/Salvatore et al, 2026-07-27).** MS lists reduced tokenmaxxing as **"Concern #1"** behind the July AI-infrastructure selloff and rejects it: "**The data suggest that this is not a valid concern.**" The three legs: **(i) Ramp estimates find the current median token spend by Enterprise users is extremely low — under $11/month**; **(ii) the economics are "deeply in the money"** — in a study of enterprise AI use cases "the average single use would save an Enterprise customer **$55 in costs**," against a token cost MS builds bottom-up as **$2-5** (a query ≈ 5,000 tokens; an AI agent ≈ **15x** a query; 5-7 agents to execute one economic task = **375,000-525,000 tokens**; at **$3-10 per million tokens** = $2-5); **(iii) "Enterprise failure to adopt the most advantageous AI capabilities will lead to large competitive disadvantages, a dynamic we expect to become increasingly clear over time."** MS's summary line: "**We disagree with the 'tokenmaxxing' argument that Enterprise limits on token spending will suppress AI revenue — we see the opposite playing out.**"
  - **★ SIDE B — MICROSOFT'S IR SAYS IT IS ALREADY HAPPENING, AND FASTER THAN THEY EXPECTED (UBS callback with MSFT IR, 2026-07-30).** Karl Keirstead: "**Jonathan was clear that Microsoft customers are indeed changing their behavior. And he said internally at Microsoft, they've been surprised at how quickly their enterprise customers have shifted from token-maxxing to token-optimizing** [or 'token-minning']." The offset, also from IR: "**any drag on Azure growth as a result of token spend optimization was offset by other tailwinds.**" Note UBS had flagged this exact risk into the print and reports "**this topic didn't even come up**" on the call itself — so it surfaced only in the callback, which is why the day-one desk flow does not carry it.
  - **HOW TO HOLD BOTH: they are not actually contradictory, and the reconciliation is the useful output.** MS is arguing about the **aggregate spend level** (median enterprise token spend is trivially low and the ROI is 10-25x, so budgets should rise); Microsoft is describing a **change in buying behaviour at the margin** (customers routing to cheaper models and trimming waste). Per-task cost can fall while total spend compounds — that is Jevons, which MS explicitly invokes. **The falsifiable question is whether optimisation outruns adoption in any given quarter.** Microsoft's answer for F4Q26 was no (Azure accelerated to +43% cc anyway). ⚠️ **But note the mix caveat from the same callback: the June GitHub Copilot repricing to usage-based pricing produced +60% q/q GitHub Copilot revenue and UBS calculates it alone "could be over a full point of Azure upside in the September quarter" — so part of the "adoption beat optimisation" evidence is a price change, not a volume change.**
  - **MS's supporting mechanism — orchestration layers are the institutionalisation of token-optimising, and MS treats them as bullish, not bearish:** "we expect to continue to see a range of LLM advances that result in better efficiencies (an example being **the rise in the usage of 'orchestration layers' that allocate work to the most cost-effective LLM**)… **Importantly, this relentless pursuit of efficiency (by both Chinese and American LLM developers) reinforces our fundamental view that demand for compute is likely to vastly exceed supply.**" ⚠️ **This is precisely what [[MSFT]] is now doing first-party with MAI as the cheap tier (see [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md): "90% of the tasks are done by the Cyber Flash One model and 10% of the tasks you still go to the frontier"), and what [[AMZN]] said on 07-30 it intends to do with its own frontier model in Bedrock. Orchestration is bullish for compute in aggregate and bearish for the frontier labs' share of that compute — the page should keep those two conclusions separate.**
  - **The token-deflation arithmetic that makes MS comfortable (Intelligence Factory model):** a **Blackwell data center earns ~60% net margin on token sales, Rubin ~80%, Feynman ~90%** — so **"hyperscalers could price tokens 75% lower [Blackwell → Feynman] and achieve the same margins on a leased data center."** Falling token prices are therefore margin-neutral for the operator and volume-accretive, which is the supply-side half of the Jevons case. MS also runs its model on Kimi K3: a **200MW data center operating with Kimi K3 rather than the extant frontier model sees only a ~3% increase in margin** — i.e. the cheap-Chinese-model shock is much smaller in operator economics than in headline price. (Morgan Stanley "Playing the AI Infrastructure Dip", 2026-07-27) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Morgan_Stanley_700HK_Global_Thematics_Playing_the_AI_Infrastructure_Dip_Whe.html) · [UBS callback](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_30_callback_2q26_msft_ubs.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-29) — 🔴 THE TOKENMAXXING QUESTION PUT DIRECTLY TO A HARDWARE MANAGEMENT TEAM, AND THE ANSWER IS A REFUSAL OF THE FRAME — WHICH IS THE MOST USEFUL ANSWER THIS PAGE HAS COLLECTED FROM THE PHYSICAL LAYER (Aurelion Research / PhotonCap "A Conversation with Lumentum", 2026-07-29).** ⚠️ **Provenance, because it decides the weight: this is a JOINT INDEPENDENT publication, not a bank. The management meeting and investment analysis were prepared by Aurelion Research (who met Kathryn Ta, VP of Investor Relations at [../LITE.md](../LITE.md)); the technical comments are PhotonCap's. The authors state interview responses are paraphrased from their notes and "do not represent verbatim statements by Lumentum or its management."**
  - **THE QUESTION, WHICH IS THIS PAGE'S THESIS ASKED VERBATIM (Aurelion Research):** "Our view, and it is still an emerging one, is that **we are compute constrained as a society and may become more so. As models like Kimi K3, GPT 5.6 and the latest Grok get cheaper per token, elastic demand should push total token consumption higher rather than lower.** How does Lumentum think about that? Does it shape capacity expansion or the innovation around data center efficiency?"
  - **THE ANSWER — MANAGEMENT, AND THIS IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING: "Lumentum is at the 'plumbing' layer of the data center, two or three steps removed from any token economics. The number they optimise is power efficiency per bit, and the route to it is higher power lasers. The more bits moved per square area of indium phosphide, the more value delivered. Speed and efficiency are the same problem to them; faster transmission is how joules per bit comes down"** (Lumentum mgmt via Aurelion Research, 2026-07-29). **On capacity, the same answer in units: "they are adding as fast as they can. Two fabs in Japan produce most of the world's supply of EML lasers, and capacity there is up eight-fold in two and a half years… Lumentum now runs five fabs, including a new one starting to ramp that is not yet producing wafers."** **Read the refusal literally rather than dismissively: management is saying the token-price curve does not enter its planning function at all — the optimisation target is joules per bit, and it happens to move in the same direction as cheaper tokens. That is a cleaner statement of the physical-layer's relationship to token economics than any of the vendor-side Jevons claims already on this page (MU 07-27, Hock Tan 07-07, SK's Chey Tae-won 07-10), because it does not invoke Jevons at all — it just denies the transmission channel exists at its level and then optimises the one variable that matters.**
  - **AURELION'S "THOUGHTS" — WHERE THE INCENTIVE ALIGNMENT ARGUMENT LIVES (this is the authors' analysis, not management):** "**Efficiency as the optimisation target is the right thing to be chasing. Hyperscalers are working on cost per token and that pressure runs all the way down to the 'plumbing'. The useful part is incentive alignment. Higher power and higher speed make the link more efficient and carry higher ASP at the same time. Lumentum gets paid to build what its customers actually need.**" ⚠️ **That is a genuinely different claim from the deflation-narrative on the rest of this page: at the plumbing layer, the cost-per-token squeeze is not a margin threat but an ASP driver, because the thing that lowers joules per bit is the higher-priced product. Log it as the counter-example to the "token-price deflation compresses the whole stack" reflex — the compression is real at the model layer and inverts at the physical layer.** Aurelion's flag on the capacity number is retained too: "**Eight-fold EML capacity in two and a half years cuts against a pure scarcity thesis. Lumentum is racing to relieve the bottleneck that gives it pricing power. We read that as confidence.**"
  - **THE VALUE-MIGRATION CHAIN, SPELLED OUT LAYER BY LAYER — AURELION, AND IT IS TIGHTER THAN THE USUAL "VALUE MOVES TO INFRA" LINE.** "**The cost per unit of model intelligence is persistently decreasing, especially with new open source releases like Kimi K3 and Grok 4.5. If demand for tokens is elastic, which we posit it is, then consumption rises faster than price falls. So, more competitive cheaper-per-intelligence models increase aggregate demand for compute while moving margins away from frontier models. Models would, in this case, become increasingly commoditized, with high competitive pressure and low switching costs.**" Then the part this page normally leaves at a high level: "**Combined with open-weight models compressing pricing, value migrates toward layers that depend on physical scarcity. This is a widely held conclusion, however, it is normally left at a high level.**" Their ranking of scarcity by layer: **"Leading edge foundry capacity is arguably the most scarce layer in the entire supply chain, with TSMC being effectively the sole frontier supplier. HBM is also constrained across 3 global suppliers, and behaves as a commodity, with more capacity expected to come online within the next 18 to 24 months. Optical and photonic components are currently in a similar position to memory in terms of viable suppliers and industry barriers to entry, given the underlying material science in indium phosphide fabrication and reliability requirements."** ⚠️ **Note the HBM framing — "behaves as a commodity, with more capacity expected to come online within the next 18 to 24 months" — is a more cautious mark than the DRAM-as-the-Jevons-trade thesis this page carries from Chris Wood (06-25) and the [../MU.md](../MU.md)/[../SKHYNIX.md](../SKHYNIX.md)/[../SAMSUNG.md](../SAMSUNG.md) rows. Both stand.**
  - **THE HONEST CAVEAT, AND IT IS THE ONE MOST OFTEN SKIPPED: A MIX SHIFT TO INFERENCE IS NOT 1:1 GOOD FOR THE PHYSICAL NETWORK LAYER.** Aurelion: "**training is the most communication intensive workload, and the way that optics have traditionally traded is around training economics. Inference is less network intensive, or at least has been historically, so a mix shift toward inference does not translate to a 1:1 demand for optics. But, as inference architecture evolves, this gap may close with things like longer context windows, more intense routing, disaggregated prefill and decode, and higher agentic usage.
