Inference is also more geographically distributed, which will drive more data center interconnect, regardless of cluster-to-cluster linkage.**" Net: "**Optics at large are insulated from model commoditization, because they benefit from bits moved rather than tokens generated across infrastructure.**" **PhotonCap supplies the live evidence that the gap is closing, with the bound intact: "This gap is already closing. The joint inference solution AMD and Cerebras announced in July is the live example. Helios handles the front half, reading the prompt, and Cerebras handles the back half, generating the answer, so work in progress crosses a network between two machines. What that link is made of has not been disclosed. So this is evidence that inference moves a lot of data, not evidence of any vendor's revenue"** (PhotonCap technical comment, 2026-07-29). ⚠️ **"Bits moved rather than tokens generated" is the cleanest one-line statement of how the physical layer is insulated from model commoditisation that this page carries — but it is exactly why the training→inference mix shift needs watching rather than assuming, and the disaggregated-prefill/decode leg is the same mechanism the SemiAnalysis 07-24 CUDA-moat work identified from the software side.** See [../LITE.md](../LITE.md) and [optical-cpo.md](optical-cpo.md) for the hardware leg. [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/A_Conversation_with_Lumentum.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — 🔴 THE FULL BOTTOM-UP TOKEN P&L, THREE BUSINESS MODELS DEEP, STANDARDISED ON ONE GIGAWATT — THIS IS NOW THE REFERENCE GRID FOR THIS PAGE (Morgan Stanley "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC" · Brian Nowak, CFA, with Stephen Byrd and Adam Wood, Internet | North America, Industry View Attractive, 2026-07-27).** A different note from Byrd's "buy the dip" piece logged below and a materially bigger contribution: **Nowak's team builds three separate GenAI ROIC models rather than arguing the ROIC question rhetorically.** **Every framework is standardised on the same physical unit: 1GW of NVDA GB300 = 410,256 chips, 3.6bn GPU-hours/yr, $39bn all-in capex/GW, $7.6bn/yr opex/GW, 75% utilisation, 21% tax.**
  - **MODEL 1 — HYPERSCALER GPU RENTAL (IaaS). Base case $8.50/hr → revenue $22.9bn/GW, incremental EBIT $15.3bn, ~67% incremental EBIT margin, NOPAT $12.1bn, ~31% ROIC.** The scenario band on price alone: **$7.0 / $8.0 / $8.50 / $9.0 / $10.0 per hour → revenue $18.9 / $21.6 / $22.9 / $24.3 / $27.0bn, margins 60 / 65 / 67 / 69 / 72%, ROIC 23 / 28 / 31 / 34 / 39%.** MS's stated range for the business is **60-70% incremental EBIT margins and 25-40% ROIC.**
  - **MODEL 2 — MODEL-ENABLED API ON OWNED INFRASTRUCTURE. Base case 65% of capacity used for inference, 2,750 tokens/sec/GPU, $1.75 per million tokens (blended) → revenue $30.4bn/GW, incremental EBIT $22.7bn, ~75% margin, NOPAT $17.9bn, ~46% ROIC.** The band runs **19% → 63% ROIC** across the 50-80% inference-share / 3,500-2,000 tokens-per-sec / $1.00-$2.50 grid. **Note the shape rather than the endpoints: revenue is $17 / $26 / $30 / $34 / $39bn while TOKENS PER YEAR barely move (16,981 / 17,466 / 17,345 / 16,981 / 15,525bn) — because MS deliberately walks throughput DOWN as it walks price UP. The whole revenue range is a pricing range, not a volume range.**
  - **MODEL 3 — MODEL-ENABLED API ON 3P (RENTED) INFRASTRUCTURE. Base case adds a $7.75/hr GPU rental → revenue $40.5bn/GW against ~$28bn of compute cost, incremental EBIT $12.6bn, ~31% margin, NOPAT $10.0bn, ~25% NOPAT margin.** The band is the widest and the most instructive: **−9% to 32% NOPAT margin, with the low case actually LOSS-MAKING (−$2.5bn EBIT at $1.00/mn tokens against a $7.00/hr rental).** ⚠️ **MS labels this "ROIC" as illustrative — it is revenue adjusted for training capacity less the rental cost of compute, taxed at 21%, and it is NOT computed on an asset base. Do not put it in the same column as models 1 and 2.** Revenue/GW also looks flattering here because **MS does not apply the 75% utilisation haircut to the 3P case** (its own disclosure).
  - **🔴 THE METHODOLOGY IS THE PART THAT MATTERS MOST FOR THIS THEME, AND IT DEMOLISHES THE MOST COMMON MISUSE OF A TOKENS/SEC NUMBER.** MS: **"we remind investors this is not solely driven by chip architecture. Token throughput varies by model parameters, software stack, token input/output mix, etc. Our illustrative range of 2,000-3,500 tokens/second/GPU is based on industry data specifications for a DeepSeek-V4-Pro 1.6T, FP4, 8K-input/1K-output workload at a specified interactivity level of ~50 tokens/second/user."** **So the 2,000-3,500 band is a WORKLOAD SPECIFICATION, not a hardware constant — model size, precision, I/O mix and the per-user interactivity target are all baked in, and MS says explicitly it treats throughput "as a range rather than a permanent, fixed characteristic of the hardware."** The corollary is the sharpest single sentence in the note for anyone modelling revenue off bigger models: **larger models (MS names 2.8T Kimi-K3) "have higher token pricing than our illustrative example… they also experience lower per-GPU throughput. As a result, HIGHER PRICING ON LARGER MODELS DOES NOT NECESSARILY TRANSLATE PROPORTIONATELY INTO HIGHER REVENUE/GW."** **Direct read-across to the Kimi-K3 debate that has dominated this page since 07-17: model capability and revenue per gigawatt are not the same axis.** Pricing benchmark used: **Muse Spark 1.1 blended $1.58 per mn tokens (8K/1K I/O)** — consistent with the ~$1 Meta Muse mark already on this page.
  - **HOW TRAINING IS CHARGED, WHICH IS THE OTHER METHODOLOGICAL CHOICE WORTH KNOWING: MS does not model a training cost line. It "discount[s] the amount of compute available for revenue generating inference by a given percentage… we assume a percent of compute is allocated toward training that generates no revenue, but bears all the costs (compute depreciation/rental costs, powered shell depreciation costs, energy and other opex)."** That is why the **65% inference share** is the single most powerful lever in Model 2 — the other 35% is a full-cost, zero-revenue block. **A cleaner way to charge training than an opex add-back, and it means MS's ~46% ROIC is already burdened with a 35% training drag.**
  - **MS'S OWN CONCLUSION, AND IT IS NOT AN UNQUALIFIED BULL CASE: "These healthy potential returns are also an invitation for competition, which speaks to how we should expect further new entrants/open source offerings to emerge…which will only further 1) keep an importance on product and compute efficiency innovation and 2) highlight the value of compute infrastructure assets."** **Read for this page: MS is arguing that attractive token ROIC is self-limiting at the model layer and self-reinforcing at the infrastructure layer — the returns get competed away from whoever sells the tokens and capitalised into whoever owns the gigawatts.** Bullish AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT (infrastructure) and GOOGL, META (models). Capex/GW decomposition on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md); the power/shell leg on [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md). [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/INTERNET_20260727_2024.html)
  - **Interactive rebuild (2026-07-30)**: all three models as live-formula xlsx at `P:\Felipe Monteiro\Semis MAP\MS_GenAI_ROIC_1GW_v1.xlsx` (Models 1-3 + base-case summary tab; blue = MS inputs, verified vs every reported figure within rounding). Two fixes vs the print: Model 2's "Tokens per Year (bn)" row is actually TRILLIONS (label corrected), and Model 3's mid-column rental rates ($7.50/$8.00) are Capstone interpolation — MS anchors are $7.00 low / $7.75 base / $8.50 high.
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — THE JEVONS ARGUMENT STATED EXPLICITLY BY A MEMORY VENDOR, PLUS A SUPPLY-SIDE CAVEAT THAT CUTS AGAINST A POPULAR BEAR DATAPOINT (Micron IR call · Satish, 2026-07-27).** Asked directly whether new architectures could reduce memory per chip, Satish gives the full chain: **"they could do various techniques you might have seen in the press — sparse attention or compression or mixture of experts. These are popular techniques that are used to reduce the amount of hardware need[ed], it could reduce the amount of memory you need, it could reduce the amount of compute you need in order to produce the output tokens. And that is a very important part of improving the economics of the tokens to increase the adoption of tokens. And it drives two things: it drives more users to use it if the prices of the token go down, which increases demand. Another factor… is to expand the context, so you can use agentic workflows more aggressively, for example, to do more useful work. And so these are things that collectively are called Jevons paradox and we believe in that as well — that reduction or improvement in economics of tokens leads to a net positive in terms of overall demand."** **Note the second mechanism, which is the less-cited one and the more interesting: cheaper tokens do not only add users, they let EXISTING users expand context and run agentic workflows harder. That is intensity growth on a fixed user base — a different and more durable demand vector than adoption growth.**
  - **⚠️ AND THE CAVEAT THAT SHOULD BE READ AS A CORRECTION TO A BEAR DATAPOINT CIRCULATING ON THIS PAGE: falling memory content per server right now is a SUPPLY CONSTRAINT, NOT AN OPTIMISATION.** Satish: **"right now, we don't have enough suppl[y] in [the] industry… when the customers want to increase the amount of servers that they want to ship, then the numerator memory content is not increasing, server units are increasing faster. So the ratio decreases — the content is expected to grow at a slower rate compared to what customers were previously saying because of the supply constraint. But it's, we think, more a supply related issue as opposed to an optimization that truly reduces the amount of memory you need per unit of hardware."** **The customers-cutting-memory-content datapoint is real, but it is rationing, not efficiency — and it reverses when supply arrives.** ⚠️ Vendor-sourced and self-serving; logged as Micron's argument, not as a finding. [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_27_mu_ir_call_27_jul_26.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-28) — the supply chain's own read on the AI-inflation worry, in one line: "Despite the market's AI inflation concern recently, in our view, THE TOKEN COST IS STILL A MORE IMPORTANT FACTOR IN DRIVING CSP'S CAPEX, and we remain positive about AI market demand" (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates" · Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang, 2026-07-28).** Worth logging because of where it sits: **Fubon writes it immediately after estimating that HBM cost roughly DOUBLES in 2027 and that the Rubin GPU price "will skyrocket to US$78-80K."** **So the claim is not that input costs are benign — it is that CSPs underwrite capex off cost-per-token, not cost-per-accelerator, and will absorb a doubling in HBM cost as long as tokens/dollar keeps improving.** That is the same mechanism MS prices in Model 2 above from the demand side, arrived at independently from the supply chain. The BOM detail sits on [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md). [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Our_advanced_packaging_CoWoS_forecast_adjustments_other_up.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — the cleanest token-unit-economics framework yet, and it is the core of MS's 'buy the dip' call. Stephen Byrd's team reads the AI-infrastructure selloff as PRIMARILY TECHNICAL, NOT FUNDAMENTAL. On enterprise budgets: 'MEDIAN CURRENT ENTERPRISE TOKEN SPEND IS LESS THAN $11 PER USER PER MONTH, UNDERMINING CONCERNS THAT CORPORATE TOKEN BUDGETS ARE ALREADY BECOMING RESTRICTIVE. MS estimates A TYPICAL AI-ASSISTED ENTERPRISE TASK CAN GENERATE $55 OF LABOR SAVINGS FOR ONLY $2-5 OF TOKEN EXPENSE' — assuming 5-7 agents, roughly 15x more tokens per agent than a basic query, 375K-525K total tokens, and $3-10 per million-token pricing.** **On the supply side, the generational margin ladder is the number to carry: 'AI token infrastructure economics improve sharply by chip generation — BLACKWELL TOKEN-SALE MARGINS APPROXIMATELY 58-60%; RUBIN ROUGHLY 75-80%; FEYNMAN APPROXIMATELY 90%. MOVING FROM BLACKWELL TO FEYNMAN COULD ALLOW TOKEN PRICES TO FALL APPROXIMATELY 75% WHILE MAINTAINING EQUIVALENT MARGINS.'** (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd via Tom Wigg, 2026-07-27)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — MS's direct answer to the Kimi-K3 disruption read, and it is quantified rather than rhetorical: 'the debate around Chinese models such as KIMI K3, GLM-5.2, DEEPSEEK, BABA QWEN AND MINIMAX is seen as MORE NEGATIVE FOR US FRONTIER-LAB ECONOMICS THAN FOR INFRASTRUCTURE DEMAND. Better and cheaper models should EXPAND INFERENCE USAGE THROUGH JEVONS PARADOX.' And specifically: 'KIMI K3 DOES NOT APPEAR TO BE A LOW-COMPUTE “MIRACLE”. It is characterized as a LARGE, CAPABLE AND RELATIVELY EXPENSIVE FRONTIER MODEL. RUNNING K3 IN A 200 MW FACILITY IMPROVES MODELED MARGINS ONLY APPROXIMATELY 1-3 POINTS VERSUS THE PRIOR FRONTIER-MODEL ASSUMPTION.'** **That 1-3 point figure is the most useful number available for sizing the K3 shock — it prices the efficiency gain as marginal at the facility level, against a market that repriced the memory complex by 5-15% on it.** Corroborating from the demand side: **@deedydas observed K3 reaching #10 ON OPENROUTER AT ~140B TOKENS/DAY WITHIN TWO DAYS, WITH SERVING INFRASTRUCTURE DEGRADING — THROUGHPUT DOWN FROM 30 TOK/S TO 13 TOK/S AND END-TO-END LATENCY UP TO ~72s** — i.e. the observable first-order effect of a 'more efficient' model was capacity failing to keep up with adoption. (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd via Tom Wigg, 2026-07-27; @deedydas corpus, 2026-07-19)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — the counter-signal, and it should be weighted: enterprises are reported to be economising. 'US COMPANIES FLIPPED FROM “TOKENMAXXING” TO “THRIFT-MAXXING”' (WSJ, via MS TMT desk).** Set directly against MS's <$11/user/month median enterprise token spend, the two are not necessarily contradictory — low absolute spend is consistent with deliberate frugality — but **the behavioural direction is the opposite of what the Jevons argument assumes, and it is a first-party report on buyer conduct rather than a model of buyer economics.** Related model-pricing marker the same day: **[[ANTHROPIC]] launched CLAUDE OPUS 5, which 'comes close to Fable 5 performance at half the price… priced at $5/1M INPUT TOKENS AND $25/1M OUTPUT, SAME AS OPUS 4.8'** — i.e. a capability step at unchanged headline pricing, which lowers effective cost per unit of work without a list-price cut. (Morgan Stanley · Tom Wigg, 2026-07-27)
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the enterprise VERBATIMS behind the token-optimisation call, from ~a dozen Microsoft customer/partner conversations plus Evidence Lab survey work — this is the differentiated content and it is more useful than the conclusion (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis "Microsoft Corp. — Update on Azure, Capex and M365", UBS Evidence Lab, 2026-07-26).** **UBS's operative claim for this page: "capacity constraints on external customers and EARLY TOKEN OPTIMIZATION EFFORTS COULD LIMIT THE POTENTIAL FOR MATERIAL AZURE GUIDANCE UPSIDE" — i.e. token optimisation has crossed from a thesis about frontier-lab revenue into a line item capping a hyperscaler's guide.** UBS frames the behaviour plainly: after enterprises "leaned heavily into AI use and experienced unexpectedly-high AI token costs in 1H, 2H is increasingly being marked by a more rationale spending approach, to optimize token consumption and eliminate unnecessary spend, in many cases by **pivoting to less expensive open source models for non-frontier tasks**." **The down-tiering quote to carry, because it puts a share number on the substitution: a customer running Copilot alongside Claude, OpenAI and Gemini, whose spend per developer went "$100 → $200 → an average of $1,500 a person… within 6-7 months" and total spend "$23,000 to $100,000 a month," concludes — "there's this line where giving AI to everybody is not the right solution… YOU DON'T NEED THE STATE OF THE ART MODELS ALL THE TIME. I DON'T NEED OPUS 4.8 TO DO ALL THE WORK. I CAN GO DOWN TO MAYBE OPENAI MINI FOR 90% OF WHAT I DO. Model routing is mainly manual and about educating people today."** **The disintermediation quote, which is the one with structural consequences for [[MSFT]]: "We are diversifying our AI partners, moving away from our Microsoft centricity, really just for risk management. We need to be multi-model and are NOW TALKING DIRECTLY WITH ANTHROPIC AND OPENAI. On token spend, IT IS UP MATERIALLY TO BE SURE, BUT WE ARE NOT OVERTLY OPTIMIZING YET, WE ARE TRYING TO PUSH AI ADOPTION. Rather, we are trimming elsewhere in our budget to make room, mostly by tempering headcount growth but also by pulling back on our spending growth with external IT services partners. We have internally-built model routing features… routing non-frontier tasks to non-frontier models."** **Read that one carefully: token spend up materially, optimisation NOT yet underway, and the funding source is headcount and IT services — which is a substitution OUT of labour and services budgets, not a cut to AI.** Corroborating from the same checks: **"customers are looking at personas and workflows to assess token guardrails… credits per user per month limits"; "costs are ballooning and customers are getting a lot more pushback from sourcing to control it. IT'S STILL RELATIVELY EARLY because a lot of customers who are using it are not the heavy users… a lot of our customers are not creating token-maxxing dashboards"; "consumption pricing is one of the biggest concerns we have… even if we push back, THESE VENDORS DO HAVE BIG COSTS ON THEIR END, SO THIS COST WILL FLOW TO US"; and one CIO — "we got our first bill and leadership said 'WE DON'T HAVE THE MONEY FOR THIS'."** **UBS's own judgement runs the other way on the infrastructure layer, and it is worth stating in full because it is the cleanest version of the argument: the consensus view "is that these trends could be A NET POSITIVE FOR AZURE GROWTH (AND THAT OF AWS AS WELL)" because (a) "an AI model market dominated by three frontier lab giants (Anthropic, OpenAI and Google) CREATES RISK FOR THE HYPERSCALERS, with too much economic power concentrated in too few customers, while A MORE DIFFUSE AND COMPETITIVE MULTI-MODEL MARKET DIMINISHES THAT RISK," (b) Azure and AWS "are already platforms offering access to a host of models, with CLOUD-HOSTED INFERENCE COMPUTE REQUIRED WHATEVER MODEL IS SELECTED," and (c) lower AI costs "may drive more AI tasks and hence be at least net neutral or better."** ⚠️ **But UBS also names the specific revenue line at risk, which this page had not isolated: "AZURE AS WELL AS AWS AND GOOGLE CLOUD ARE NOW MATERIALLY MONETIZING OPENAI, ANTHROPIC AND GOOGLE MODEL API ACCESS, as well as IP product demand, and any material customer effort to optimize token consumption and route to open models COULD IMPACT THE GROWTH OF THESE REVENUE STREAMS IN 2H."** So the hyperscalers are not model-agnostic in the way the neutral read implies — they carry a first-party API-resale book that is directly exposed. **The counter-evidence UBS itself supplies, and it is the strongest single datapoint against a visible token-deflation drag: "This was certainly NOT EVIDENT IN GOOGLE CLOUD'S 2Q26 RESULTS (GROWTH ACCELERATED TO +82%) but much of the excessive enterprise token spend appears to be in CODING USE CASES FOR WHICH GEMINI MODELS ARE LESS DOMINANT."** The caveat is doing real work — it is why +82% is not yet proof either way. Also logged: **Microsoft's high OpenAI exposure is the transmission channel** ("a strong majority of Microsoft's AI revenues are derived from OpenAI and selling access to OpenAI models/products, and a very large portion of the committed backlog is derived from OpenAI"), which UBS calls "a key reason why the shares of Microsoft, Oracle, CoreWeave and other AI infra providers have been weak since early-June"; and a third-order lever — **"one lever (among many) to fund higher-than-expected AI token costs is to OPTIMIZE ELSEWHERE IN THEIR AZURE/AWS SPEND."** (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis "Microsoft Corp. — Update on Azure, Capex and M365", 2026-07-26; the capex/FCF leg on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md); see [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../OPENAI.md](../OPENAI.md), [../ANTHROPIC.md](../ANTHROPIC.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/ued48088.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — 🔴 THE SAME-BENCHMARK COST TABLE, which is the number this page has needed and is very different from sticker pricing (JPM · Samik Chatterjee / Arti Vula / Jaiden Patel, "Software Weekend Edition: Navigating Token Costs Through Federated Routing, SLMs, and Open-Weight Models," 2026-07-26).** **Sticker rates (per million input/output tokens, company websites as of 7/21/26): Anthropic Fable 5 $10.00/$50.00 — top of the market · Opus 4.8 ~$5.00/$25.00 · OpenAI gpt-5.6-sol ~$5.00/$30.00 (≤272K input) · SpaceXAI Grok 4.5 $2.00/$6.00 (≤500K).** Dated items: **Sonnet 5 runs introductory $2/$10 through 8/31/26, reverting to $3/$15 on 9/1/26** (a ~50% effective increase on Sep 1); **Opus 5.0 shipped 7/24/26 at $5/$25**, excluded from JPM's table pending third-party benchmarks. **But "headline pricing rates… may tell only part of the story as the 'all-in' cost to run the Artificial Analysis (AA) Intelligence Index makes the divide look wider, since models consume very different token volumes to reach the same benchmark, i.e. to complete the same task": Fable 5 $5,631 · Opus 4.8 $3,753 · Kimi-K3 $2,710 · qwen3.7-plus $311 · deepseek-v4-flash $74.** So on a same-task basis Fable 5 costs **~2.1x Kimi-K3, ~18x qwen3.7-plus and ~76x deepseek-v4-flash.** JPM's caveat: point-in-time snapshot, subject to provider revisions and new frontier releases.
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the finding that contradicts the popular narrative, and it is the more strategically important half of the same note: using Epoch AI's ECI score, JPM compared the quarterly average of the top three open-weight vs top three closed-weight models over ~3 years and found that "CONTRARY TO THE POPULAR INVESTOR PERCEPTION, the performance difference of the top closed- and open-weight models has remained relatively stable over the last couple of years, rather than materially converging."** Open weights are **cheaper, not catching up.** JPM's read: still "a material positive for software companies that can plan to leverage open-weight models as part of their long-term product strategy with reassurance around performance keeping up," while conceding the same cost advantage "lowers a meaningful barrier to entry for smaller software companies or in-house software development" — with platform companies less exposed than point-product ones.
- **⚠️ NEW (2026-07-26) — THE CACHE REBUTTAL, and it may collapse most of the apparent gap. It comes from a house bearish nearly everything else in AI, with a first-person measurement (R&Co Redburn · Alex Haissl, "Margin Call" roadshow feedback, 2026-07-26):** "This is about **cost-per-task, not the sticker price of tokens**. The big pushback is that Chinese open-weight models (e.g. Kimi K3, GLM-5.2) now rival the closed frontier at a fraction of the headline price, which should crush token economics. **We think that overstates the real gap: Modern agentic workloads are dominated by cached tokens** — reused context (system prompt, conversation history) that is read from memory rather than recomputed via token spend. **In Alex's own Claude Code usage, he found stored-cache reads were ~98% of tokens, with fresh input under 2%.** Those open-weight models also **repeat failed tasks such as trying to go to a blocked website multiple times, while frontier models don't**. So while open-weight models look far cheaper on headline sticker pricing, **they're only 2x cheaper when you adjust for cache.**" **The reconciliation worth noticing: JPM's own all-in Fable-5-vs-Kimi-K3 ratio is ~2.1x — essentially Redburn's "2x."** So the two houses may agree on the frontier-vs-Kimi gap, and **the genuine dispute is over the CHEAP TAIL (qwen ~18x, deepseek-v4-flash ~76x).** If that is right, the frontier labs' exposure is not to Kimi-class models at all — it is to the very cheap tier absorbing high-volume simple work, which is precisely what the routing architectures below are built to do.
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — federated routing and SLMs are now "table stakes," with named implementations (JPM, same note):** **Zoom** — "a clear, relatively mature example: its patent-pending federated architecture employs a lower-cost LLM first, evaluates output quality via its proprietary **Z-Scorer**, and escalates to a more advanced model only when required"; **Microsoft — Foundry Model Router**, routing across a configurable subset of OpenAI, Anthropic and other models, plus an explicit **family of small models** described as often "much cheaper or much faster" per request; **Intuit — GenOS**, orchestrating across Claude and GPT by query type and system capacity; **Salesforce — Agentforce** (managed model mix); **Cloudflare — monetizes the trend directly, selling dynamic routing via AI Gateway**; and **Snowflake, ServiceNow, Adobe and Oracle** offering customer-directed model choice ("a precursor architecture"). On SLMs: **ServiceNow has its own open-weight reasoning model and the line of the week — "people are realizing you don't need to take a Ferrari to mail a letter"**; Adobe is building purpose-built small models for creative/brand workflows; Intuit custom-trains domain models for tax and accounting. JPM's framing: these are **additions to the arsenal, "not replacements for frontier LLMs,"** serving as the low-cost tier — and collectively the reason software gross-margin compression "can moderate."
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — down-tiering as OBSERVED behaviour rather than forecast (WSJ, via Vital Knowledge): "CTOs around the world are pushing back against exploding AI costs, utilizing lower-cost, open-source models from China for most tasks and reserving only the most specialized and complex queries for OpenAI and Anthropic."** Note this is compatible with frontier-lab revenue growth **if** the retained queries are the high-value ones — which is exactly what is unresolved, and is the same question behind the UBS token-optimisation note (07-22) and the Rothschild/TickerTrends ARR datapoint (07-24) on [[ANTHROPIC]]. UBS's own four-part conclusion (Keirstead/Arcuri, 07-26): open models "could weigh on the growth rates of the frontier labs, but the consensus view was that AI spending growth is so robust/early-stage that frontier as well as open models could both win. **Investors are now on edge for any update on OpenAI and Anthropic growth**"; at the GPU layer demand is **reallocated, not reduced — "bullish Nvidia"**; hyperscalers are already multi-model so **"the share price risk is more from the high frontier lab exposure"**; and it is a modest headwind for software sentiment because customers "may react to rising AI token costs by throttling apps/services spend."
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — real-world token burn at a non-tech enterprise, which is the demand side of all of the above (BofA · Brian Fenske, TMT Idea Bank, quoting AT&T): "We're burning about a trillion-plus tokens per month, and annualizing now roughly 16.4 trillion tokens. AT&T has more than 100 generative AI models in production (approx. 40% are running AMD hardware)… AT&T transcribes more than 300,000 customer calls per day."** And the same effect showing up as a P&L line at **SAP**: "you also see that the costs are more up than the headcount. **That is actually the token effect, because we charge the tokens, of course, to the functions who are using it**… we are also now improving the efficiency and improving the bang for the buck, **introducing model routing technologies and so forth**." Two more: **UNH spending $1.5B explicitly on AI in '26** with "virtually every provider and consumer interaction uses AI"; and on the productivity side, **GOOGL — "83% of Alphabet's sales team uses Gemini-assisted tools weekly, leading to up to a 20% higher win rate"** and **SHOP — AI-search traffic "converted at nearly 50% higher rates" than organic with 14% higher order values, AI-driven traffic +8x y/y and orders from AI +13x.**
- **NEW (2026-07-24) — the inference moat has MOVED: from single-node CUDA kernels to disaggregated prefill/decode + WideEP, and the model trend makes it structural (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24).** The most important framing datapoint for token economics in this batch: SemiAnalysis argues "the competitive frontier has moved from single node aggregated to multi node disaggregated inference" — *"WideEP, disaggregated prefill, KV transport, expert routing, and scheduler/orchestration are all part of that same new moat"* — so inference leadership is now **"a distributed-systems problem rather than a single-kernel one."** The model side is what makes it structural: **active expert counts stay pinned in the 4-10 range while total experts scale out to 128, 256, 384 and now 512**, with many new giant MoEs collapsing the KV cache to the equivalent of one or two heads via MLA-style low-rank designs — *"the frontier is getting sparser, more bandwidth-sensitive, and more dependent on routing, KV movement, WideEP, and distributed scheduling."* The payoff is quantified: on DeepSeek-R1 (256 routed experts) moving from EP8 on one 8-GPU node to **EP64 across 64 GPUs cuts experts per GPU from 32 to 4**, freeing HBM for KV cache and scaling aggregate HBM bandwidth with cluster size; NVIDIA reports **up to 2.28x higher per-GPU output throughput from Wide-EP** (DeepSeek-V3.2, NVFP4, GB200 NVL72, EP16/EP32 vs EP4/EP8). Disaggregated prefill's cost is KV-cache transfer over RDMA — SemiAnalysis estimates **~290MB for an 8,192-token DeepSeek-R1 FP8-KV prefill**. Read for the theme: **the unit cost of a token is now set at the cluster/topology layer, not the chip layer** — which is why the cluster-TCO/goodput framework backfilled below matters more than any single-node benchmark, and why "frontier labs and most TaaS providers are already deploying disaggregated serving with WideEP in production." (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24; the AMD-specific software-gap leg sits on [../AMD.md](../AMD.md), the rack/optics leg on [optical-cpo.md](optical-cpo.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_24_ENC_Can_AMD_break_the_CUDA_Moat_AMD_Advancing_AI_2026_email.html)
- **BACKFILL — HISTORICAL, dated 2025-08-20 (ingested 2026-07-24; ~11 months old, do NOT read as current) — the cleanest independent record of *training* cost per token, and of how much of the deflation is pure software (SemiAnalysis "H100 vs GB200 NVL72 Training Benchmarks — Power, TCO and Reliability Analysis", 2025-08-20).** This page has plenty of *inference* cost-per-token marks but almost no training ones; this backfill fills the hole with measured benchmarks. **Software-only deflation on fixed hardware:** training GPT-3 175B on 128 H100s, the FP8 cost fell from **72 cents to 54.2 cents per million tokens trained between Jan-2024 and Dec-2024** at $1.42/hr/GPU — purely from CUDA-stack software (**BF16 MFU 34%→54%, a 57% throughput gain; FP8 MFU 29.5%→39.5%, +34%**) — taking a 300B-token run from **$218k to $162k**. **Absolute training cost:** Llama 3 405B pretraining over 15T tokens cost **$1.95/M tokens in BF16 on a 2,304-H100 cluster = $29.1M for the pretraining run alone**, which SemiAnalysis calls "dramatically higher than mixture of expert models such as DeepSeek – which cost only $5M per training run." **Hardware-generation deflation:** DeepSeek 670B BF16 on GB200 NVL72 went **$0.684/M tokens (May-25) → $0.307/M (Jul-25)** vs H100 at $0.468 — a 1.5x perf-per-TCO advantage — implying a **$4.5M pretrain on 14.8T tokens as of Jul-25, projected to $2.5M by Dec-25**; Llama4 Maverick 400B MoE FP8 at **$0.061/M vs $0.093/M on H100**. **Energy per token (the physical floor under the token curve):** **2.46 J/token FP8 and 3.63 J/token BF16** for GPT-3 175B on Dec-2024 software, **8.8 J/token for Llama3 405B**; the average US household's annual **10,791 kWh** would train **15.8B FP8 tokens** of GPT-3 175B, and training Llama3 405B to convergence on 15T tokens needs the annual energy of **~3,400 US households**. **The MoE structural point that pre-dates and explains the 2026 WideEP moat above:** H100 BF16 MFU on DeepSeek 670B is only **16.6%** versus **54.5%** on dense Llama 3 405B, because of expert dispatch/combine communication scaling O(top_k) — and the GB200 NVL72's 72-GPU scale-up world size makes **64-GPU all-to-all collectives 18x faster** than on 64 H100s, which is precisely why it shines on wide-EP MoE inference. (SemiAnalysis "H100 vs GB200 NVL72 Training Benchmarks", 2025-08-20 — **historical backfill; every number is a 2024/2025 mark superseded by later hardware**; the power leg is on [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md), the connector leg on [cowos-packaging.md](cowos-packaging.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2025_08_20_ENC_H100_vs_GB200_NVL72_Training_Benchmarks_-_Power_TCO_and_Reliability_Analysis_Software_Improvement_Over_Ti.html)
- **BACKFILL — HISTORICAL, dated 2026-04-20 (ingested 2026-07-24; ~3 months old, pricing inputs are an Aug-2025 snapshot) — the definitive cluster-TCO / "goodput" framework, and the margin math that decides whether a token business makes money (SemiAnalysis "How Much Do GPU Clusters Really Cost?", 2026-04-20).** The missing denominator for every cost-per-token mark on this page: **cluster TCO = GPU + storage + network + control plane + support + goodput + setup + debugging** — i.e. the sticker $/GPU-hr is not the cost. Headline: **holding $/GPU-hr equal, a gold-tier neocloud is 5-15% cheaper on TCO than a silver-tier one** across large training workloads, collapsing to ~zero for fault-tolerant single-node inference. Three 3-year scenarios: **5,184 GB300 NVL72 large-LLM pretrain → gold 1.00x / hyperscaler 1.10x / silver 1.15x, with goodput expense 6.14% / 10.53% / 20.91%**; **2,048 B200 multimodal RL → 1.00x / 1.61x / 1.15x**; **512 H200 inference endpoints → hyperscaler 1.59x**, with goodput differences worth only ~0.5% of TCO (why inference providers can safely rent lower-tier capacity). MTBF assumptions behind it: gold and hyperscaler ~**25,000 GPU-hr** vs silver ~**15,000**; failure identification 15 min vs 1 hr. **The margin math that matters for anyone selling tokens:** at a **$3/M-token blended selling price**, a ~20% rise in H200 goodput expense from **$1.90 to $2.10/hr/GPU cuts gross margin from 28% to 20%**, and at **$3.10/hr GM goes to -18%** — derived from **326 tok/s/GPU** H200 throughput at 75% effective utilisation = **0.88M tokens/hr**. In other words the 1.59x hyperscaler cost premium "translates almost directly into margin compression." **Fault tolerance is the hidden lever:** TorchFT is the only open-source option and is not widely adopted; TorchFT, HyperPod Checkpointless and TorchPass all carry communications/memory/idle-node tradeoffs, leaving fault-tolerant *training* **"a secret sauce available to frontier labs or those willing to pay for a software license,"** while fault-tolerant *inference* is already standard for single 8-way systems. **ClusterMAX 2.1 (April 2026)** added Core42, BitDeer, FPT Smart Cloud, Radiant/Ori, Tatra, QumulusAI, Boostrun, Moonlite, Vessl, SK Telecom and BytePlus — with **two providers (FPT and Ori) held out of silver for PKey/SAKey misconfiguration that let SemiAnalysis see every other tenant's endpoints on the network**. Note the pricing direction at the time: **GPU rental prices were going up** as of April 2026. (SemiAnalysis "How Much Do GPU Clusters Really Cost?", 2026-04-20 — **historical backfill, Aug-2025 price inputs**; the build-vs-rent leg is on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_20_FW_How_Much_Do_GPU_Clusters_Really_Cost_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-24) — the token number is now quantified, and inference has passed training: ~35 quadrillion tokens/month (~160x growth in two years), with inference at ~60% of AI compute in 2026 (AMD "Advancing AI 2026" keynote via @PatrickMoorhead, 2026-07-23; corroborated in the MS and BofA recaps, 2026-07-24).** Lisa Su's opening framing puts hard numbers on this theme's core claim: **monthly token consumption ~35 quadrillion, ~160x growth in two years**; **inference demand now exceeds training at ~60% of AI compute in 2026**; and **agentic AI (agents that plan, call tools, iterate) is driving a step-change** requiring **both high-performance GPUs for model work and a great deal of CPU for orchestration** — the mechanism behind AMD raising its CY30 server-CPU TAM to **>$220bn** with **agentic AI ~50% of the TAM and the CPU:GPU ratio tightening toward 1:1** (from 4:1 today). Sam Altman independently frames the same shift from the model side: *"I think we have not invested enough in delivering cheap intelligence at scale. **all of frontier labs are gonna be inference companies** to a certain degree"* (via @Hesamation, 2026-07-23) — alongside [../OPENAI.md](../OPENAI.md) raising its **2030 capex forecast to ~$750bn**. **Cost-per-token continues to fall fast, which is the deflationary half of this theme:** AMD claims **MI455X delivers 34x higher throughput and 18x lower token cost vs MI355X** (@sssjeffpu, 07-24), and BofA cites **SemiAnalysis/InferenceX showing MI355X at up to ~40% lower cost-per-token than NVDA B200 on SGLang FP8**. Also new on the tooling side: **AMD's rocm.ai** lets **Claude, Codex and Cursor understand AMD hardware natively** and automate kernel tuning (38% tok/s gain optimising MiniMax M3 on MI355; **2.4x training / 3.3x inference vs ROCm 7**), with Anthropic's Tom Brown on stage saying Claude largely bootstrapped the MI355 bring-up unaided — "**we are past the CUDA moat days**" (@austinsemis, 07-23). See [../AMD.md](../AMD.md), [../CEREBRAS.md](../CEREBRAS.md).
- ⚠️ **NEW (2026-07-24) — a second, independent question mark over frontier-lab growth, and this one is on the revenue line: "TickerTrends data suggests Anthropic ARR sequential growth has slowed significantly" (Rothschild & Co · Rowan Adley, TMT Daily, 2026-07-24).** Carried with the analyst's explicit caveat — *"I have no idea if this data is legit"* — but he names exactly why it matters for this theme: **"an air pocket in Frontier Labs growth has been exactly what the market has been worrying about (the open vs closed-source debate)."** **This is unverified alt-data and nothing else corroborates it** — Yipit had Anthropic ARR at **$69B with daily adds accelerating $400M→$550M (07-10)**, SemiAnalysis at **>$60B for 3Q26 with NNARR $10B+/month (07-08)**, Dylan Patel at **>$50B (07-10)**. But it lands three days after the **UBS "first dents" call** already at the top of this page (token-spend optimization + open-source routing as the first real headwinds), so **there are now two independent reads questioning frontier-lab growth rate or quality where a week ago there was one** — one on unit economics, one on the top line. Possible market footprint: Adley links it to the 07-23 tape (**GOOG -6.9%, NDX -1.9%**, with **Asian memory very weak and the KOSPI -5.7%** on no memory-specific newsflow). **If an unverified private-company ARR read can move the Korean memory complex, that is itself a statement about how load-bearing tokenmaxxing has become for the entire AI-capex trade.** Top item to verify at the next hard datapoint. See [../ANTHROPIC.md](../ANTHROPIC.md), [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md), [hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md).
- **NEW (2026-07-23) — AMD frames the demand curve as "a complete compute picture": token usage rising every month (volume and quality), with HBM content central to token economics (Morgan Stanley · AMD earnings transcript/ET, 2026-07-23).** On its print, AMD management gave a demand-side token read to pair with the supply-side Jevons math on this page: **token usage is growing "every single month" — not just the number of tokens but the quality of output AI delivers** — and AMD "really think[s] about AI as a complete compute picture… every aspect of compute" (CPU + GPU + memory), pushing back on a narrow GPU-only framing. It also flagged **HBM content as "a very important part of… token economics,"** noting some peers cut HBM content on memory availability (the memory-quantum/de-speccing thread — see [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md)). Anchors the token-demand-growth side as AMD's Helios/MI450 ramp wins Anthropic/OpenAI/Meta (custom-vs-merchant detail on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md)). (Morgan Stanley · AMD ET, 2026-07-23; see [../AMD.md](../AMD.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/AMD_-_ET_CP_document_dated_23-07-2026.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-22) — the "first dents" call: UBS says token-spend optimization + open-source routing are the first real headwinds to Frontier-Lab (OpenAI/Anthropic) revenue — but Arcuri argues it is a Jevons *positive* for semis (UBS "Open-source models & token spend optimization", Kiersted/Arcuri/Rupel, 2026-07-22).** The most direct sell-side statement yet that the two 2026 trends this theme tracks — **(1) token-spend optimization** post the H1 enterprise-AI-adoption inflection (enterprises reining in "material and in some cases excessive" use of OpenAI GPT / Anthropic Claude Opus frontier models), and **(2) a pivot to cheaper/more-token-efficient models, often open-source Chinese, for non-frontier tasks** — are "the first few potential dents to the revenue growth trajectories of the Frontier Labs." Sourced from ~15 private AI-natives at UBS's 5th annual private-AI conf (ClickHouse, Cognition, Cursor, 11 Labs, Factory, Firework, Sierra, Harvey). Key marks: **(a) the AI-natives' growth is still extraordinary** — Firework past $1B rev (from $400M Jan), Cognition $500M ARR (+7x y/y), ClickHouse >$250M (3x YTD), Higgsfield $500M (~0 fourteen months ago), Perplexity >$500M (3x YTD) — Kiersted's evidence enterprises are "leaning into AI," most of it invisible in public-software P&Ls; **(b) "value maxing" is now dominant** — a real concern for a majority of enterprises installing controls (Cursor: "every single customer conversation is about this now"), though not everyone throttles (ClickHouse Claude spend $20k Jan → $1M Jun, +50x = ~5% of revs, *not* throttling — "they see value"); **(c) the dominant lever is dynamic routing of non-frontier subtasks to cheaper models** (Cognition splits a single inference job into subtasks across models) — open-model token share inflecting (Factory: ~0.1% Dec → 1% Feb → **10-15% now**, "could be 90% in 12 months"; **Decagon ~30% of token requests**), winners the **Chinese labs (GLM/Z.ai [up ~800% YTD], Kimi/Moonshot, DeepSeek, Qwen/Alibaba)** plus **Nvidia's Nemotron** as the most-cited non-Chinese open model; **(d) the market has passed into a multi-model, commoditized (fragmented, not perf-converged) end-state — "winner-take-most is highly unlikely."** Frontier-lab read: OpenAI's/Anthropic's **2028-30 revenue targets "could feel more best case now"** and at minimum erode investor confidence ("frankly, that's what's getting priced into tech stocks"; cites Altman's "we did not have our best last 12 months ever" tweet); Anthropic-specific — **some of its extraordinary H1 growth "likely came from the use of Opus 4-class models for non-frontier tasks"** now being eliminated/routed to cheaper Chinese models. Likely lab responses Kiersted flags: ship more token-efficient models (a GPU/TPU pull-through), move up-stack into software (bad for the software sector), avoid a Chinese-lab price war, and lobby DC for regulatory FUD around open-weight Chinese models (Dean Ball/OpenAI tweet). **Semis / hyperscaler read-through (Tim Arcuri, 5 points) — the Jevons bull:** (1) cheaper models → *more* token demand → **good for NVDA, not bad** ("I would push back pretty hard" on the AI-capex-negative read); (2) the incoming **Blackwell-trained frontier models are far more token-efficient** (Jensen's ~50x cost reduction, "a ton in the hopper"); (3) **China is short compute** so cheap models get hosted where compute is — and the **Broadcom SPV with OpenAI + Anthropic = ~20GW over 2yrs (7GW in 2027 + 13GW in 2028, 2028 skewing ~8-9GW to Anthropic)**, demand "way more than that 20 gigs," gated only by financing / DC build-out; (4) **Nvidia is the open-source-model vendor outside China (Nemotron)** and is vertically integrating (procuring fiber, backstopping DC deals); (5) **Kimi K3 is a huge model — very bullish for memory** (only servable on GB300 / MI450 HBM-dense racks → good for Micron). Carl's **hyperscaler** read: a fragmented/competitive model market is **neutral-to-positive for AWS/Azure** (Chinese-model deployment *increases* cloud-hosted-compute demand, per Moonshot/Databricks), but names with **outsized OpenAI exposure (MSFT/Oracle/CoreWeave/AI-infra) carry the nervousness**; **software** is a mild negative (AI-cost crowding out headcount/IT-services/app spend; medium-term risk the labs move up-stack). Best risk/reward: **Arcuri — NVDA, AMD, Broadcom (thinks the Google/MediaTek debate is better for AVGO than the market fears), Micron, semicap**; **Kiersted — Palantir** (model-agnostic, no tokens through the P&L). Reinforces the recurring "cheaper/open models *expand* the token base (Jevons) while re-routing where the value accrues" pattern the page tracks — the frontier keeps the high-margin revenue only if the SOTA-vs-open gap stays wide. (UBS "Open-source models & token spend optimization", 2026-07-22; see [../OPENAI.md](../OPENAI.md), [../ANTHROPIC.md](../ANTHROPIC.md), [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [china-export.md](china-export.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_22_ubs_open_source_models_and_token_spend_optimizatio.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-20) — MS/Byrd "Bitcoin-to-DC Conversions": Blackwell→Feynman lets hyperscalers "price tokens ~75% lower and achieve the same margins"; DC net token-sale margins 58-90% by GPU generation (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd, 2026-07-20).** Byrd's bitcoin-miner-to-datacenter-conversion note restates the supply-side Jevons math the theme tracks: moving Blackwell→Feynman, **hyperscalers "could price tokens ~75% lower and achieve the same margins,"** and he sizes **data-center net token-sale margins at 58-90% depending on GPU generation** — i.e. even as list token prices fall dramatically the compute layer keeps very high margins gen-over-gen. Corroborates/extends the MS Byrd "Discussion on tokenmaxxing" (2026-06-26) ~75%-cost-drop mark already on this page. (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd "Bitcoin-to-DC Conversions", 2026-07-20) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Bitcoin_Miners.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-20) — Citrini "All Along the AI Watchtower": near-memory compute = the "detour around the HBM toll booth"; Meta Muse blended token pricing ~$1; aggregate token spend "growing rapidly" (Citrini, 2026-07-20).** Citrini frames near-memory compute (e.g. QCOM HBC) as the **"detour around the HBM toll booth"** — an architecture that monetizes tokens with fewer HBM bytes as HBM prices climb — and marks **Meta's Muse blended token pricing at ~$1** with aggregate token spend **"growing rapidly,"** consistent with the low-priced-Muse-distributor and Jevons threads on this page. (Citrini "All Along the AI Watchtower", 2026-07-20; the HBM-substitution leg is on [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md) / [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Citrini_jul_20.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-20) — Rothschild/Redburn "Hyperscalers — Margin Call": inference specialists Baseten/Fireworks each process more tokens/min than the OpenAI or Google APIs; Baseten NDR ~400%; Bedrock wins 32%/23% of DeepSeek-V3.2 / gpt-oss-120b provider comparisons (Rothschild & Co Redburn · Haissl/Han, 2026-07-20).** A hard datapoint on how token demand is fragmenting toward the inference-serving layer: **Baseten (~$13bn) and Fireworks (~$17.5bn) each process more tokens/minute than the OpenAI or Google APIs**, with **Baseten net dollar retention ~400%**; on open-weights, **AWS Bedrock wins 32% / 23% of provider comparisons for DeepSeek V3.2 / gpt-oss-120b** — the middleware/cloud layer capturing serving share as open models proliferate. (Rothschild & Co Redburn · Haissl/Han "Hyperscalers — Margin Call", 2026-07-20) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Redburn_Research_4.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-17) — Barclays/Sandler "Charts Worth a Look": AWS Bedrock's ~35% operating-income margin is INVARIANT to serving open- vs closed-source models — the cloud layer is margin-agnostic as token demand fragments (Barclays · Ross Sandler, 2026-07-17).** Barclays quantifies why the hyperscaler layer wins regardless of who wins the model war: **AWS Bedrock's operating-income margin is ~35% whether it serves an open- or closed-source model** (Kimi 2.5 ~35% vs Anthropic Sonnet 5 ~36%), because the margin is driven by **batch rate / interactivity, not the model** — Kimi 2.5 GPU margin runs **28% at 120 tok/s/user → 71% at 70 tok/s/user**. Read: the cloud/serving layer is margin-agnostic to model provenance and *benefits* as token demand fragments away from the frontier labs — the cleanest support yet for the "hyperscaler-neutral / net-positive on open-source share" thread (Sandler's 07-17 K3-momentum counter already logged below). (Barclays · Ross Sandler "US Internet — Charts Worth a Look", 2026-07-17) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/20260717_Barclays_US_Internet-_Charts_Worth_a_Look-_What_Happens_To_Hype.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-15) — Octahedron 2Q26 LP call: Chinese open-weights "~90% of the capability at ~20% of the cost," but the frontier duo keeps a "~6-month" lead + harness lock-in; "commodities at scale are spectacular businesses" (Octahedron, 2026-07-15).** A fund-view read on the commoditization debate: Chinese open-weights (**GLM 5.0, Qwen 3.6/3.7, MiniMax**) offer **"~90% of the capability at ~20% of the cost,"** and **OpenAI is waging a high-quality-token price war** — but the frontier duo keeps a **"~6-month" lead** and converts it into **tool-use/harness lock-in (Claude Code, Codex)**. Conclusion: some commoditization is real but **"commodities at scale are spectacular businesses"** — a nuanced middle between the "open-source deflates the frontier" bear and the "frontier keeps ~90% of the value" bull already on this page. (Octahedron 2Q26 LP call, 2026-07-15) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_15_octahedron_2q_review.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-19/20) — Kimi K3 follow-through: #10 on OpenRouter (~140B tok/day) with infra "crumbling," 75% more expensive per unit of intelligence than OAI's model, Moonshot pauses new subs + plans an HK IPO (Jefferies · Beavington; UBS/Reuters; FinTwit, 2026-07-19/20).** The K3 aftermath confirmed both the demand pull and the capacity strain the theme tracks. Demand: **K3 hit #10 on OpenRouter within 2 days at ~140B tokens/day**, but its **infra is "crumbling" — throughput fell 30 → 13 tok/s, E2E latency up to 72s** (@deedydas); **Moonshot temporarily paused new subscriptions after demand strained capacity** (Reuters via UBS/Arcuri) and is **planning a Hong Kong IPO within ~6 months** (@zerohedge). Economics: @zephyr_z9 pegs K3 at **2.8T total params (≈1.4T served at fp4), the sparsest frontier model at ~1.7% activation, ~75-85% GM on inference** — i.e. a cheap-to-serve sparse MoE. But Jefferies/Beavington's key cost mark cuts against the "cheap Chinese model" panic: **K3 is ~75% more expensive per unit of equivalent intelligence than OAI's (slightly more intelligent) model** — the frontier still wins on cost-adjusted capability. @DavidSacks amplified the policy angle (K3 "fixed 15 critical security bugs Codex/Fable refused on guardrails" — arguing US models handicap themselves). Read: reinforces the theme's core — cheaper/efficient open models *expand* the token base (Jevons) and bump into GPU-capacity limits fast, rather than deflating the compute-demand curve. (Jefferies · Beavington, 2026-07-19/20; UBS/Reuters, 2026-07-20; @deedydas/@zephyr_z9/@DavidSacks/@zerohedge, 2026-07-19/20; see [china-export.md](china-export.md), [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md))
- **NEW (2026-07-18) — Kimi K3 pricing marks + UBS/Arcuri "token maxxing is NOT a headwind for semis" (UBS · Ruple/Arcuri; Jefferies · Rankie Wong; Bernstein · Seidman, 2026-07-17/18).** The K3 pricing detail sharpens the commoditization debate: **Kimi K3 priced $3/$15 per 1M input/output tokens** — cheaper than the leading Western closed models (GPT-5.6 Sol $5/$30, Fable 5 $10/$50) but a **big price hike vs its own predecessor K2.6 ($0.95/$4)**, and only comparable to the *step-below* Western tier (GPT-5.6 Terra $2.5/$15, Opus 4.8 $5/$25, Grok 4.5 $2/$6, Sonnet 5 $3/$15, Meta Muse Spark 1.1 $1.25/$4.25). Bernstein frames it as **40% cheaper than Opus 4.8 / 70% cheaper than Fable** but expects models to commoditize while "products/services built on top matter more." At the **UBS Private Software & AI conference, Tim Arcuri concluded token maxxing is NOT a headwind for Semis** — strong AI demand + accelerating open-source adoption is ultimately a *positive* dynamic (Jevons rerun of the post-DeepSeek fizzle), particularly for **NVDA**, whose software leadership in open-source models (Nemotron called out favorably) is an under-appreciated edge. Read: the "cheaper Chinese model → price compression" bear is the same DeepSeek reflex; on-disk consensus is that K3 *expands* the token/compute base and the frontier keeps the high-margin revenue. (UBS · Robert Ruple/Tim Arcuri, 2026-07-18; Jefferies · Rankie Wong, 2026-07-17; Bernstein · Tyler Seidman, 2026-07-17; see [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [macro-cycle.md](macro-cycle.md))
- **NEW (2026-07-17) — Moonshot AI's Kimi K3 = "DeepSeek 2.0?" triggers an AI-momentum unwind; open-source-token-share the debate (BofA · Fenske; MS · Wigg; Bernstein · Seidman; Barclays · Ross Sandler, 2026-07-17).** Beijing-based Moonshot AI's new **Kimi K3** model — landing the same week as a reported **GOOGL Gemini 3.5 Pro delay** — drove a sharp risk-off: **SOX −4% on 07-17 (now a −23% drawdown for 2026), Nasdaq −2.1%**, the "fastest/deepest momentum sell-off on record" (MS/Wigg). The bear bundle: **peak-memory, peak-capex-expectations, peak-positioning + distillation fears** (a strong cheap open-weight model → negative read to frontier labs GOOGL/META). Barclays/Ross Sandler's counter: **open-source tokens taking share from frontier labs is net-positive for hyperscalers long-term** — the cloud layer is broadly agnostic on unit economics and escapes frontier-oligopoly pricing pressure. Read for the theme: this is the recurring "DeepSeek moment" pattern — a cheap open model spikes distillation/commoditization fear, but (per the frontier-keeps-the-revenue and Jevons threads below) cheaper/more-capable open models historically *expand* total token demand while re-routing where the value accrues (model layer vs hyperscaler platform). BofA/Fenske: "I remain both bullish and wrong" — framing it as risk-management-over-fundamentals positioning, not a demand break. (2026-07-17; see [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../META.md](../META.md), [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [macro-cycle.md](macro-cycle.md), [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md))
- **NEW (2026-07-16) — MS's inaugural Edge AI survey: inference is expanding beyond the hyperscalers, but AWS/Azure still dominate today (Morgan Stanley · Sanjit Singh "Edge AI Survey", 2026-07-16).** A survey of ~40 resellers/partners frames where AI inference actually runs today: **AWS 68% / Microsoft Azure 37% / Cloudflare 29% / Google Cloud 21%** are the top platforms for executing AI inference — i.e. the two hyperscaler leaders (AWS, Azure) remain the primary destinations for centralized inference, but specialized edge providers ([NET](../NET.md) Cloudflare, [AKAM](../AKAM.md) Akamai) are taking incremental share of *edge* inference (Cloudflare 55% share-gainer / 63% top vendor for edge compute+inference; 31% of partners see enterprise edge-inference adoption in 2027). Read for the theme: as inference (vs training) becomes the marginal workload, it fragments across a wider vendor set — hyperscalers keep centralized/large-context inference while latency-sensitive/real-time inference migrates toward edge, a modest dis-concentration of the token-serving base. MS raised **NET PT to $322** (from $305) on the read. (Morgan Stanley · Sanjit Singh et al., 2026-07-16; see [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/SOFTWARE_20260716_1110.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-16) — MS 2Q26 CIO Survey: where the GenAI spend dollars are actually being allocated (Morgan Stanley · Josh Baer "2Q26 CIO Survey Takeaways", 2026-07-16).** The AlphaWise CIO survey (n=100) puts hard vendor marks on GenAI-spend allocation. **Incremental GenAI-spend share (2026): Microsoft 27% (down from 32% in 1Q26), LLM Providers 11%, Amazon 10%, Salesforce 8% (up from 7%), Google/ServiceNow 6% each; on a 3-year view Microsoft 30%, LLM Providers 17%, Amazon 12%, Google 11%, Salesforce 8%, ServiceNow 5%** — LLM providers (the frontier labs) are the emerging secondary share-gainer, taking budget share from the hyperscalers on both the 1-yr and 3-yr views. **Agentic-automation vendor preference: Microsoft 36%, Google 8%, Amazon 7%, LLM Providers 7%.** 97% of CIOs expect to use some Microsoft AI tooling over the next 12 months (up from 92%); M365 Copilot penetration 88% (up from 80%); GitHub Copilot 58% (up from 42%). Read for the theme: the "LLM Providers as an emerging share-gainer" datapoint is the CIO-survey corroboration of the frontier-labs-capture-the-value thesis — token/API spend is starting to route to the model layer directly, not only through the hyperscaler platforms. (Morgan Stanley · Josh Baer, 2026-07-16; see [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../CRM.md](../CRM.md), [../NOW.md](../NOW.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/MICROSOFT_20260716_0224.html)
- **Agentic-commerce framing (older, 2026-05-28) — the transaction verticals must build their own agent front-ends; AI-referred traffic still tiny today (BofA · Justin Post "Agentic AI and the promise of a more capable Internet", 2026-05-28).** BofA's agentic-commerce thematic frames the monetization stakes for the transaction Internet (eCommerce, online travel, finance): conversational AI is becoming the primary discovery/decision interface (**Google AI Mode now ~1bn users**), so marketplaces must build competitive front-end AI + downstream agentic capabilities to keep direct traffic. But AI-referred traffic is still de-minimis today — **ChatGPT drove just ~0.42% of Amazon's total web traffic in May'26, ~0.36% for Booking** — so the disintermediation risk is a forward one, not a current-quarter drag. Google's **Universal Cart / UCP** (persistent cross-merchant cart; merchants incl. Shopify, Etsy, Wayfair, Target, Walmart + Visa) and **Booking's Penny agent** (broader agentic rollout targeted 2H26) are the early build-outs. Ratings in the note: **GOOG Buy PO $430** (already on the GOOG page); the note's BKNG PO $150 predates BKNG's split and is superseded by the current $208 (JPM). Additive agentic-commerce context for the token/AI-monetization theme. (BofA · Justin Post/Nitin Bansal, 2026-05-28; see [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../BKNG.md](../BKNG.md), [../SHOP.md](../SHOP.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/BAML_agentic_commerce_28_may_26.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-14) — Redburn's demand-side sizing: AI revenues $789bn (2025) → $2trn (2028) "justifies 100GW of capacity at current monetisation rates" — the timing of monetisation vs infrastructure is the whole risk (Rothschild/Redburn · Karamanos "AI Monetisation & Sector Rotation", 2026-07-14).** The clearest top-down monetization framework on the page. Holding **Anthropic's ~$20bn of revenue per 1GW constant** and assuming **enterprise IT budgets double from ~4% of sales** (leading adopters already spend 2-10%), Redburn derives **~100GW of justified capacity ≈ ~$4-5.5trn of capex — roughly the "AI-11's" 2024-32 spend.** The thesis behind the recent sector rotation: over the past 6 months software & media raised 2027 capex + leverage while RoCE/FCF fell and sales-growth expectations stayed flat — investors increasingly worry about monetisation timing. The bear tail: **hyperscalers could pull capex back (lifting their own cash flow/shares) while spooking the data-center supply chain** that may have over-estimated demand — especially any player that funded expansion by over-leveraging. Also frames "token-maxing early in the year" as **a symptom of inefficient token use + inaccurate budgeting during the experimentation phase**, not structural demand — consistent with the enterprise-cost-control thread below. (Rothschild/Redburn · George Karamanos, 2026-07-14; NA top Buys incl. Lam Research, Lumentum, Coherent, Amphenol) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Redburn_Research_3.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-14) — JPM's 2Q-preview framing: token pricing has fallen "pretty dramatically," which JPM reads as a *positive* (→ increased enterprise adoption); enterprise margin concern real but offset by efficiency + model flexibility (JPM · Anmuth "US Internet 2Q26 Preview" + 2Q preview call, 2026-07-14).** JPM splits the token-pricing debate by lens: for **enterprises/corporates**, increased AI expenditure drives margin concerns (Uber the loudest, others vocal) but JPM sees it **driving greater efficiencies, in some cases better monetisation, more model flexibility ("sufficient-enough" models, not always the most expensive), and some employee-level capping**; for the **AI labs**, token pricing coming down "pretty dramatically" should **over time mean increased enterprise adoption of AI**. Frontier-model competition is accelerating — Claude Fable 5 / Opus 4.8, GPT-5.5 / 5.6 Sol, Grok 4.5 (SPCX/Cursor), Muse Spark 1.1 (Meta), Gemini 3.5 (Google) — with corporates looking to offset via smaller/sufficient models incl. open source. (JPM · Doug Anmuth, 2026-07-14; see [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../META.md](../META.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md)) [PDF](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/JPM_JPM_ANMUTH___US_Inte_2026-07-14_5366760.html) · [call](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_14_jpm_preview.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-13) — the "capacity wars," a new token-as-a-service vendor category, and GLM 5.2 as the first credible open-source codegen model (Archera expert call — ex-AWS operator, 2026-07-13).** A dense supply/demand read from an ex-AWS operator. (1) **"Capacity wars":** GPU/CPU capacity dried up through 1H26 — hyperscalers now force 1-year (not 3-year) commitments and charge for A100 access; average price/H100 across the operator's book up **~25-30%** (NeoCloud list even to ~$230-240/hr), with production workloads bleeding to NeoClouds/Oracle/DigitalOcean. (2) A **new "token-as-a-service" category emerged in Q2** — Together AI, Poolside, Base10, Cloudflare Workers now serve **order-millions of dollars of production tokens** for real ISVs/digital-natives (esp. non-proprietary open-source models). (3) On the model mix going to **production, ~60/40 open-source:closed tokens** (but internal R&D still ~80%+ proprietary Claude/GPT); **Claude ~70/30 over GPT** after the GPT-5.5 resurgence; **data agents are "the new coding agents."** (4) **GLM 5.2 is the first credible open-source codegen model** (~¼ the cost of Opus, having hit the RL loop) — if the pattern holds, the operator sees open-source potentially taking ~80% of codegen tokens over time and squeezing the labs "into 20%." US middleware (Bedrock/Vertex/Foundry, Together, Base10, Cloudflare) taking responsibility for the model is what makes enterprises comfortable adopting Chinese open-source. (Archera expert call, 2026-07-13; see [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../ANTHROPIC.md](../ANTHROPIC.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_13_archera_13_jul_26.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-14, companion podcast) — Nowak reiterates Meta Muse API priced 30-80% below the private labs + AWS incremental rev/watt only $7-9 (Morgan Stanley · Nowak "Hyperscale capex to $1.4T", 2026-07-14).** The Morgan Stanley Tech Talk podcast (companion to the 07-12 MS "$1.4trln of Capex" note already logged above) restates the monetization marks that have to justify the capex: **Meta will offer Muse-enabled API/agent products priced 30-80% below current private-company offerings** (business agents, coding assistants, ad-campaign tools — going after ad *and* corporate-IT budgets); **AWS incremental revenue-per-incremental-watt of only ~$7-9** (the lowest of the hyperscalers, "arguably conservative" → upward bias) vs Google's ~$50/watt SpaceX rental. Corroborates/extends the 07-12 MS pricing marks; the new detail is the podcast framing of Meta's API as an aggressive low-price token/agent distributor. (Morgan Stanley · Brian Nowak, 2026-07-14; see [../META.md](../META.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_14_brian_nowak_on_hyperscale_capex_raise_to_1_4t_new_.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-12) — Jefferies frames the funding gate: "token growth must outrun price deflation for the capex cycle to stay funded"; SPCX/META can run inference *at cost* (Jefferies · William Beavington, Global SEMIS Daily, 2026-07-12).** Amid continued pricing compression, Jefferies' desk reiterates **token growth as the single most important metric to the durability of capex spend** — the cycle stays funded only if token growth outruns price deflation. Strategic-advantage angle: **SPCX and META can generate revenue from *other* businesses while running inference at cost**, an edge vs pure labs pushing toward profitability ahead of potential IPOs. (Jefferies · William Beavington, 2026-07-12; the capex-funding leg is logged on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md))
- **NEW (2026-07-13) — BofA "Core Wars": in the agentic-AI era the system bottleneck is shifting from GPU FLOPS to CPUs (tool calls, code exec, orchestration, KV-cache) — a CPU-demand tailwind on top of the token curve (BofA "Core Wars Q2 Preview", Vivek Arya, 2026-07-13).** BofA's core thesis is a direct read-through for the inference/agentic leg of tokenmaxxing: **as workloads move from training to agentic inference, the system bottleneck is increasingly not GPU FLOPS but the CPU** — which handles tool calls, code execution, orchestration and **KV-cache management** — so CPU capability becomes critical to overall system performance and to **reducing idle GPU time**. The design debate maps to the two token-economics camps: **NVDA argues highest-frequency cores matter (latency → faster tool-call completion), AMD argues throughput/concurrency (rack-scale) matters more** (BofA: "reality is likely a mix of both," and both support materially higher CPU demand in AI systems over time). Merchant AI-CPUs (NVDA Vera / ARM AGI / QCOM CPU) ramp **CY27-28**; NVDA's ~$200bn CPU TAM is scoped to **agentic/RL workloads only, not general-purpose**. Read: cheaper tokens + agentic orchestration pull *more* CPU (not just GPU/memory) into the AI system — a broadening of the compute bill the token curve funds. (BofA · Vivek Arya "Core Wars Q2 Preview: insatiable demand for servers", 2026-07-13; the server-CPU-TAM / merchant-vs-custom detail sits on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md); see [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [../AMD.md](../AMD.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Vivek_on_CPUs.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-12) — MS puts hard marks on the AI revenue streams: Meta's Muse Spark 1.1 priced 30-85% below frontier peers; every ~100MW toward Meta's API ≈ ~$8bn revenue / ~$2 of '28 EPS; AWS incremental rev/watt only $7/$9 '26/'27 (arguably conservative) (Morgan Stanley "Internet: $1.4trln of Capex", Brian Nowak, 2026-07-12).** MS's hyperscaler note quantifies the monetization side of the token economy that has to justify the $1.4trln capex. **Meta's Muse Spark 1.1 is priced $1.25/$4.25 per 1M input/output tokens — 30-85% below private frontier offerings** (vs peers at $2-5 input / $6-30 output), an aggressive price-to-win-adoption move. MS's capacity-to-revenue bridge: **every ~100MW of compute allocated to Meta's API could generate as much as ~$8bn of revenue and ~$2 (~6%) of '28 EPS** (741 racks × 72 GPUs × 4,300 tok/s/GPU × $1.58 blended token price × 75% util → ~5.4 **quadrillion** tokens/yr [unit corrected 2026-07-20 from "5.4tn": the stated arithmetic = 5.43e15 tok/yr, and ~$8bn ÷ $1.58/Mtok only closes at 5.1e15 — "tn" was a 1000x transcription slip]; 75% GM). Meta's 5 under-appreciated '28-EPS call options (Neocloud optionality, Meta AI, Search, API, Subscription) sum to ~$10 of EPS (~30% upside). On the wholesale side, MS flags **AWS incremental revenue-per-incremental-watt of only ~$7/$9 in '26/'27 as "arguably conservative"** vs GOOGL's ~$50/watt deal — i.e. realized token/compute monetization is running ahead of models. Read: the frontier-and-distribution monetization keeps compounding, with Meta entering as a low-priced token/API distributor. (Morgan Stanley · Brian Nowak "Internet: $1.4trln of Capex, 4X in Compute Capacity", 2026-07-12; see [../META.md](../META.md), [../AMZN.md](../AMZN.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md); capex leg on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/MS_full_note.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-12) — JPM: agentic-AI server CPUs the breakout demand vector (155% CAGR 2025-28), >50% of the general server-CPU market by 2028 (from <10%) — inference orchestration is the demand (JPMorgan "JPM proprietary server CPU shipment model", Hung/Hariharan, 2026-07-12).** JPM's server model isolates the agentic-inference pull: as workloads shift from training toward inference, hyperscalers deploy more general-purpose servers around accelerators (KV-cache external-storage servers + query-orchestration compute servers) to improve TCO, and **agentic-AI CPUs grow at a 155% CAGR 2025-28 — becoming >50% of the general server-CPU market by 2028, up from <10% last year**. JPM attributes this year's strong 30-40% YoY general-server growth "primarily to the emerging demand for agentic AI." A hardware-demand confirmation of the tokenmaxxing/test-time-scaling ("long thinking") thesis — more agentic tokens ⇒ more orchestration CPUs, storage and general servers, not just GPUs. (JPMorgan · Albert Hung/Gokul Hariharan, 2026-07-12; the CPU-vendor/ARM-share detail on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md), memory pull on [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/JPM_Server_JPM_proprieta_2026-07-12_5362742.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-07) — Hock Tan: cost/token down ~70-100%/yr; per-workload XPU optimization drives cost/token/watt down; frontier shift "from selling tokens to selling products" (JPMorgan · Harlan Sur "Hock Tan @ JPM", 2026-07-07).** On the JPM call, Broadcom's Hock Tan said **cost per token is coming down ~70-100% per year** even as token generation grows exponentially. Because inference is "more diversified in architecture" (reasoning / decoding / reinforcement learning / search), the five frontier buyers tape out **more variations of XPUs optimized per-workload**, which is "what's enabled the cost per token per watt to be down." On monetization/moat, he flagged one way frontier labs defend differentiation: **"move away from selling tokens" (commoditized) "to selling products."** A supply-side corroboration of the Jevons/tokenmaxxing thesis (falling unit cost, more monetizable tokens). (see [../AVGO.md](../AVGO.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_07_hock_tan_at_jpm.html)
- **NEW (2026-06-23) — Google Principal Architect (expert call): the "inference age" (80/20 training→inference in ~3 yrs) + token-based APIs served on cheap TPUs = "a much, much better business model"; hardware refresh compressed to ~2 years (Google · Arthur Khessin, Office of the CTO, expert call, 2026-06-23).** Google's Arthur Khessin frames the demand-side substrate of tokenmaxxing: the AI compute mix shifts from **80% training / 20% inference today to 80% inference / 20% training within ~3 years**, with inference demand more **fragmented, modular and closer to consumers**. The monetization tell: as customers move to **token-based consumption (e.g. the Gemini API), the underlying hardware is abstracted away — letting Google serve those tokens on its cost-efficient TPUs and optimize margins ("a much, much better business model")** than renting raw infrastructure. Two supporting reads: **availability trumps price while capacity is sold out** (cost-efficiency becomes decisive only as the market matures), and hyperscalers have **compressed the hardware refresh cycle from 4-5 years to ~2 years** purely for power efficiency (each generation fits ~20% more compute for the same power) — a structural demand floor. Reinforces the "cheaper tokens on more-efficient silicon → more demand, with the margin accruing to whoever owns the cost-efficient stack" pattern the page tracks. (Google · Arthur Khessin expert call, 2026-06-23; the TPU vertical-integration leg on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md); see [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_23_Call_Google_-_Principal_Architect_Office_of_the_CTO_Arthur_Khessin_Memory_Cycle_2306_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-06-08) — Microsoft management explicitly rejects naive "token maxing": the goal is value-per-token, not tokens-per-customer — plan with a frontier model, execute with an off-frontier one (Jefferies/Citi MSFT non-deal roadshow, 2026-06-08).** In a MSFT investor roadshow, management named the concept directly: **"the goal for Microsoft is NOT to say let's jam as many tokens on a customer in the shortest period of time… this notion of token maxing"** — instead, in a consumptive token-based world it's about bringing value: **use the frontier model to plan the work, then get "much better value for the token throughput by doing the work with an off-frontier model."** This is the hyperscaler-side articulation of the dynamic-routing / value-maxing behavior the page's 07-22 UBS bullet quantifies (enterprises routing non-frontier subtasks to cheaper models). Supporting marks: **GitHub moved to token-based pricing** (management concedes "a touch late") in a **per-user + token-based** world; the **$190bn CY capex includes ~$25bn from increased component pricing (memory a big part)**; and **Azure Maia 200** efficiency reads "very good" as MSFT tunes workload/app patterns for margin mix. The recurring MSFT anchor: watching for when **revenue growth exceeds capex growth**. (Jefferies/Citi-hosted MSFT non-deal roadshow, 2026-06-08; see [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md); Maia leg on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_08_08_06_26_jefferies_microsoft_corporation_non_deal_.html)
- **NEW (2026-06-10 interview; ingested 2026-07-10) — SK Chairman Chey: dollar-per-token is the key AI metric, set to fall to ~1/10 within ~3 years; memory makers contributing to token-cost-down (Bloomberg TV — Chairman Chey Tae-won, 2026-06-10).** In a Bloomberg TV interview, SK Group Chairman Chey Tae-won framed **"the cost of AI" as dollar-per-token** — currently "too high" but expected to fall to **~1/10 within ~3 years** as many players (including memory makers, via better memory/KV-cache solutions) contribute to token-cost-down. He argues rising memory content in the AI BOM doesn't break the model because cost-per-token keeps falling — "there is further room for the memory BOM to increase." Geographic split: the US focuses on niche/quality tokens, China on cheaper tokens (different strategies). Reinforces the Jevons/tokenmaxxing frame this page tracks — cheaper tokens → exponentially more demand (the memory-capacity leg sits on [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md)). (see [../SKHYNIX.md](../SKHYNIX.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_10_ceo_hynix_interview_10jun26.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-10) — "Model Wars": frontier price deflation accelerates as META/SPCX undercut the labs; token growth must outrun it (Jefferies · Blayne Curtis; Citi · Heath Terry, 2026-07-10).** A week of frontier launches sharpened the price-vs-volume debate. **Grok 4.5 priced $2/$6 per MTok (>60% below Opus 4.8 / GPT-5.5); Meta's Muse Spark 1.1 at $1.25/$4.25**, with Meta management framing it "very low cost" and calling rival-lab pricing "very extreme." Blayne's key structural read: **META and SPCX can run inference at cost while monetizing other businesses — a durable advantage vs the labs (OpenAI/Anthropic) now chasing profitability ahead of IPOs** — so "token growth must continue to outrun price deflation for the capex cycle to remain funded." Yet the same week, **Citi (Heath Terry) reports average output-token prices *rising* and GPU rental prices up across Hopper/Blackwell** — demand outpacing still-constrained supply, scarcity pushing pricing up through the stack (power→semicap→memory→compute). Net: list-price deflation at the frontier tier coexists with rising realized token/rental prices in aggregate — the Jevons/tokenmaxxing thesis intact so long as volume growth dominates. (see [ANTHROPIC](../ANTHROPIC.md), [META](../META.md), [OPENAI](../OPENAI.md), [NVDA](../NVDA.md))
- **NEW (2026-07-09) — "Data is the new oil (for real this time)": the RL-environment / human-data supply chain is now a real, capital-attracting industry, and it's the third leg of the frontier moat (SemiAnalysis "The Future of Meta Superintelligence", 2026-07-09).** SemiAnalysis's MSL progress update makes the demand-side case that **RL is the dominant scaling law now** — you teach the model to complete whole tasks, which requires purpose-built environments, tools and verifiers, and "many AI insiders believe more RL environments/tasks are all we need to automate virtually all white-collar work." The invisible hand has spun up a supply chain to feed it: incumbents **Mercor, Surge and Handshake are all at $1B+ ARR**, and newer entrants barely a year old — **Fleet, Mechanize, Afterquery — are ~$100M ARR**. The unit economics show how scarce good RL data is: **frontier labs pay $5,000+ for a single decent coding RL task**; **Mercor's average pay rate recently surpassed $100/hr** (SWE rates well above; top contractors clear 7 figures/yr); **Mechanize expects a $400k+/yr SWE to make only ~1 good task/week** (the hard part is calibrating difficulty for frontier models, not labeling — the "days of bounding boxes are gone"). A key emerging input: **real screen recordings of white-collar work**, increasingly valuable for building both realistic tasks (guaranteed representative of real knowledge work) and rubric/deterministic verifiers (thousands of traces ≈ an LLM one-shotting the rubric). The demand read for the compute chain: this is why **Meta reassigned ~3,000 engineers to full-time RL task creation** and began screen/keyboard/mouse tracking — its RL-hours are already ~ **Mercor's 2,517,000 expert hours logged in 2Q26 (~4,800 FTEs)**, with ~70k more people to pull from (see [../META.md](../META.md)). Supply-node datapoint: **SpaceXAI is reportedly selling ~$26B/yr of GPUs to Anthropic/Google.** Read-through: the RL-data layer is a monetizable industry in its own right and *deepens* the frontier's data moat rather than commoditizing it — the demand-side complement to the frontier-keeps-the-revenue thesis. [../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Semianalysis_META.html](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Semianalysis_META.html)
