One State-of-play bullet block (six sub-bullets) + one Sources entry. NO rating, PT or estimate superseded.** **⚠️ ONE BENCHMARK BASIS IS EXPLICITLY DISPLACED BY THE AUTHORS THEMSELVES, and the old basis is recorded here rather than deleted: SemiAnalysis's serving benchmarks previously ran on a synthetic "8k1k / 1k1k" input/output harness; from this note they run on RECORDED INTERNAL CLAUDE CODE TRACES replayed for an hour at steady state — median 142k input tokens and 444 output tokens per turn, median 65 turns per session. Any cost-per-token figure on this page dated before 2026-08-03 rests on the older short-context basis and is not comparable like-for-like.** **The material new numbers: all OpenRouter providers floor Kimi K3 at $3/M input and $15/M output (as of 30 July 2026), against a measured serving cost of $0.1712/M input tokens on one B300 node with DSpark speculative decoding at P90 30 tok/s/user (4,844.1 tok/s/GPU input throughput, 3-year rental $2.60/GPU-hr as of July 2026) — "an order of magnitude" gap; and on the AgentX 315:1 input:output mix, Moonshot's own platform blends to $0.74/M tokens at 22 tok/s/user versus $0.171/M self-served on B300 with vLLM at more than double the interactivity, a 76.89% cost reduction. "On B200 this is much worse and nearly infeasible."** **Also recorded: cache reads are most of SemiAnalysis's own Claude Code / Codex spend (the biggest line item in a heavy agentic bill is context, not generation); Day-0 bring-up was easier than DeepSeek V4, with images and a speculative-decoder model shipped alongside the weights and Day-0 vLLM recipes from BOTH Nvidia and AMD including DRAM offload — i.e. the lag from open-weight release to cheap self-serving is collapsing; and the communication-to-computation-ratio derivation behind expert intermediate dimensions rising to 3,072 across DeepSeek V4 Pro, MiniMax M3, MiMo V2.5 Pro and Inkling, logged as convergent industry design rather than a proprietary edge.** ⚠️ **Judged and dropped: pages 1-24 of the note (KDA, attention across depth, LatentMoE, quantile load balancing, prefix-cache implementation detail) are model-architecture material with no investable read at this page's altitude — only the architecture-to-cost link is carried. The KV-cache-in-HBM / DRAM-offload hierarchy (B300 holds ~3.25M tokens of KV cache after weights; cache thrashing above concurrency 8 with hit rates <10% vs 95% theoretical; 219.91 GB/rank of CPU DRAM offload adding 15.76M tokens, 4.85x) was routed to [hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md), where the memory-content consequence belongs.** Cross-linked [[NVDA]], [[AMD]], [hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md).
- **2026-07-30 (Aurelion Research / PhotonCap "A Conversation with Lumentum", 2026-07-29 — a JOINT INDEPENDENT publication, NOT a bank). One State-of-play bullet + one Sources entry. NOTHING SUPERSEDED.** ⚠️ **Provenance carried on the page and it governs the weighting: the management meeting and investment analysis were prepared by Aurelion Research (met Kathryn Ta, VP Investor Relations at [../LITE.md](../LITE.md)); the technical comments are PhotonCap's; and the authors state interview responses are paraphrased and "do not represent verbatim statements by Lumentum or its management." Management's answer, Aurelion's analysis and PhotonCap's commentary are attributed separately.** **Why it belongs here rather than only on the company page: this is a dedicated tokenmaxxing Q&A — the interviewer asks explicitly whether models getting cheaper per token (Kimi K3, GPT 5.6, the latest Grok) and elastic token demand push total consumption higher, and how that shapes capacity expansion and efficiency innovation — not an incidental keyword hit.** **THE ANSWER IS A REFUSAL OF THE FRAME, AND THAT IS THE FINDING: "Lumentum is at the 'plumbing' layer of the data center, two or three steps removed from any token economics. The number they optimise is power efficiency per bit, and the route to it is higher power lasers. The more bits moved per square area of indium phosphide, the more value delivered. Speed and efficiency are the same problem to them; faster transmission is how joules per bit comes down." Read literally: management says the token-price curve does not enter its planning function at all — it optimises joules per bit, which happens to move in the same direction. That is a cleaner statement of the physical layer's relationship to token economics than the vendor-side Jevons claims already on this page (MU 07-27, Hock Tan 07-07, SK's Chey Tae-won 07-10), because it does not invoke Jevons — it denies the transmission channel at its own level and then optimises the single variable that matters. On capacity: "they are adding as fast as they can… two fabs in Japan produce most of the world's supply of EML lasers, and capacity there is up eight-fold in two and a half years… five fabs, including a new one starting to ramp that is not yet producing wafers."** **AURELION'S OWN ANALYSIS ADDS THE COUNTER-EXAMPLE THIS PAGE WAS MISSING: "Hyperscalers are working on cost per token and that pressure runs all the way down to the 'plumbing'. The useful part is incentive alignment. Higher power and higher speed make the link more efficient and carry higher ASP at the same time. Lumentum gets paid to build what its customers actually need." That inverts the page's default reflex — at the plumbing layer the cost-per-token squeeze is an ASP DRIVER rather than a margin threat, because the product that lowers joules per bit is the higher-priced one. Logged as the physical-layer exception to "token-price deflation compresses the whole stack." Aurelion's flag on the capacity number is kept too: "eight-fold EML capacity in two and a half years cuts against a pure scarcity thesis."** **VALUE-MIGRATION CHAIN, layer by layer rather than the usual high-level version: cheaper-per-intelligence open-source releases (Kimi K3, Grok 4.5) mean "consumption rises faster than price falls," margins move away from frontier models, models commoditise with "high competitive pressure and low switching costs," and value migrates "toward layers that depend on physical scarcity" — ranked leading-edge foundry (TSMC effectively the sole frontier supplier) > HBM (3 suppliers but "behaves as a commodity, with more capacity expected to come online within the next 18 to 24 months") > optical/photonic components ("in a similar position to memory"). ⚠️ Note the HBM mark is MORE CAUTIOUS than the DRAM-as-the-Jevons-trade thesis this page carries from Chris Wood (06-25) and in the [../MU.md](../MU.md) / [../SKHYNIX.md](../SKHYNIX.md) / [../SAMSUNG.md](../SAMSUNG.md) rows. Both stand; neither superseded.** **THE CAVEAT MOST OFTEN SKIPPED, now on the page: "training is the most communication intensive workload, and the way that optics have traditionally traded is around training economics. Inference is less network intensive, or at least has been historically, so a mix shift toward inference does not translate to a 1:1 demand for optics" — closing over time via longer context windows, heavier routing, disaggregated prefill and decode, agentic usage, and geographically distributed inference driving DCI. Net: "optics at large are insulated from model commoditization, because they benefit from bits moved rather than tokens generated across infrastructure" — the cleanest one-liner on physical-layer insulation this page carries, and simultaneously the reason the mix shift needs watching rather than assuming. PhotonCap's live evidence with its bound intact: the AMD+Cerebras July split-inference solution ("Helios handles the front half, reading the prompt, and Cerebras handles the back half, generating the answer, so work in progress crosses a network between two machines. What that link is made of has not been disclosed. So this is evidence that inference moves a lot of data, not evidence of any vendor's revenue") — the same disaggregated-prefill/decode mechanism the SemiAnalysis 07-24 CUDA-moat work identified from the software side.** Cross-filed [../LITE.md](../LITE.md), [optical-cpo.md](optical-cpo.md), [../AMD.md](../AMD.md).
- **2026-07-28 — /run-inbox, three sources. Three State-of-play bullets + three Sources entries. Nothing superseded — this page gains a reference grid it did not have.** **(1) Morgan Stanley · Brian Nowak, CFA (with Stephen Byrd, Adam Wood), "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC," 2026-07-27** — a DIFFERENT note from Byrd's "buy the dip" piece already logged 07-27, and a bigger contribution: **three bottom-up GenAI ROIC models, all standardised on 1GW of NVDA GB300 = 410,256 chips / 3.6bn GPU-hours / $39bn all-in capex/GW / $7.6bn opex/GW / 75% utilisation / 21% tax.** **IaaS at $8.50/hr → $22.9bn rev/GW, ~67% incremental EBIT margin, ~31% ROIC (23-39% band over $7-$10/hr). Model API on OWNED infra at 65% inference / 2,750 tokens/sec/GPU / $1.75 per mn → $30.4bn rev/GW, ~75% margin, ~46% ROIC (19-63% band). Model API on 3P infra at $7.75/hr rental → $40.5bn rev/GW, ~$28bn compute cost, ~31% margin, ~25% NOPAT margin (−9% to 32% band, LOSS-MAKING in the low case).** ⚠️ **Two MS disclosures logged so the three are not misread as comparable: the 3P "ROIC" is illustrative (revenue less rental cost, taxed — not computed on an asset base), and MS does not apply the 75% utilisation haircut to the 3P case, which flatters its revenue/GW.** **The methodological content is the most valuable part and is now on the page explicitly: the 2,000-3,500 tokens/sec/GPU range is NOT a hardware constant — it is a DeepSeek-V4-Pro 1.6T, FP4, 8K-in/1K-out workload at ~50 tokens/sec/user, and MS treats throughput "as a range rather than a permanent, fixed characteristic of the hardware." The corollary, with direct read-across to the Kimi-K3 debate that has dominated this page since 07-17: larger models (2.8T Kimi-K3) price higher but run slower per GPU, so "higher pricing on larger models does not necessarily translate proportionately into higher revenue/GW" — model capability and revenue per gigawatt are not the same axis.** Also logged: MS charges training by **discounting revenue-generating inference capacity while it still bears all cost**, so the ~46% ROIC in Model 2 is already burdened with a 35% zero-revenue block; token-price benchmark **Muse Spark 1.1 blended $1.58 (8K/1K I/O)**, consistent with the ~$1 Muse mark already on the page. **MS's conclusion is logged in full because it is not an unqualified bull case: attractive returns are "an invitation for competition" → more entrants/open source → returns get competed away at the MODEL layer and capitalised into the INFRASTRUCTURE layer ("the value of compute infrastructure assets").** **(2) Micron IR call · Satish (2026-07-27)** — the Jevons chain stated explicitly by a vendor (sparse attention / compression / MoE → lower cost per output token → lower prices → **more users AND expanded context / harder agentic workflows**; "collectively… called Jevons paradox and we believe in that as well"). **The second mechanism — intensity growth on a fixed user base — is the less-cited and more durable one and is flagged as such.** ⚠️ **Supply-side caveat logged as a CORRECTION to a bear datapoint circulating on this page: customers cutting the rate of memory-content growth is rationing, not efficiency — "more a supply related issue as opposed to an optimization that truly reduces the amount of memory you need per unit of hardware" — and it reverses when supply arrives.** Vendor-sourced and self-serving; logged as Micron's argument, not as a finding. **(3) Fubon (Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang, 2026-07-28)** — the supply chain's read on AI inflation: **"the token cost is still a more important factor in driving CSP's capex,"** written immediately after estimating HBM cost roughly doubles in 2027 and the Rubin GPU price reaches US$78-80K — **i.e. CSPs underwrite capex off cost-per-token, not cost-per-accelerator, arrived at independently of MS's demand-side version of the same mechanism.** Cross-filed [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md), [ai-datacenter-power](ai-datacenter-power.md), [hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md), [[GOOG]], [[META]], [[MSFT]], [[AMZN]], [[MU]].
- **2026-07-27 (b)** — **/run-inbox (theme-dossier pass). 1 State-of-play bullet + 1 Sources entry. Nothing superseded.** **UBS's Microsoft preview (Keirstead/McGinnis, 2026-07-26, UBS Evidence Lab, ~a dozen customer/partner conversations plus survey work) is the first note on this page where token optimisation appears as a line item CAPPING A HYPERSCALER'S GUIDE rather than as a thesis about frontier-lab revenue: "capacity constraints on external customers and EARLY TOKEN OPTIMIZATION EFFORTS COULD LIMIT THE POTENTIAL FOR MATERIAL AZURE GUIDANCE UPSIDE."** **The value is in the verbatims, and two are load-bearing. First, down-tiering with a share attached — a customer whose spend went "$100 → $200 → an average of $1,500 a person" in 6-7 months and "$23,000 to $100,000 a month" in total: "you don't need the state of the art models all the time. I DON'T NEED OPUS 4.8 TO DO ALL THE WORK. I CAN GO DOWN TO MAYBE OPENAI MINI FOR 90% OF WHAT I DO." Second, and structurally more important, disintermediation: "we are diversifying our AI partners, MOVING AWAY FROM OUR MICROSOFT CENTRICITY… we are NOW TALKING DIRECTLY WITH ANTHROPIC AND OPENAI. On token spend, IT IS UP MATERIALLY TO BE SURE, BUT WE ARE NOT OVERTLY OPTIMIZING YET… Rather, we are TRIMMING ELSEWHERE IN OUR BUDGET to make room, mostly by TEMPERING HEADCOUNT GROWTH but also by pulling back on our spending growth with EXTERNAL IT SERVICES PARTNERS."** That second quote cuts against the simple thrift-maxxing read logged the next day: **token spend up materially, optimisation not yet started, and the funding source is labour and services budgets — substitution out of other spend, not a cut to AI.** **UBS's own judgement is logged as the counterweight rather than netted: a diffuse multi-model market could be a NET POSITIVE for Azure and AWS, because concentration in three frontier labs "creates risk for the hyperscalers," the platforms are already multi-model, and "cloud-hosted inference compute [is] required whatever model is selected."** ⚠️ **But UBS isolates the specific exposed revenue line this page had not named: "Azure as well as AWS and Google Cloud are NOW MATERIALLY MONETIZING OPENAI, ANTHROPIC AND GOOGLE MODEL API ACCESS… and any material customer effort to optimize token consumption and route to open models COULD IMPACT THE GROWTH OF THESE REVENUE STREAMS IN 2H"** — the hyperscalers carry a first-party API-resale book and are not model-agnostic in the way the neutral read implies. **The best counter-evidence against a visible deflation drag is supplied by UBS itself: "this was certainly NOT EVIDENT IN GOOGLE CLOUD'S 2Q26 RESULTS (GROWTH ACCELERATED TO +82%)" — carried WITH its caveat, that "much of the excessive enterprise token spend appears to be in CODING USE CASES FOR WHICH GEMINI MODELS ARE LESS DOMINANT," which is why +82% settles nothing yet.** Also folded: [[MSFT]]'s high OpenAI exposure as the transmission channel (a strong majority of AI revenue and a very large portion of committed backlog), which UBS calls "a key reason why the shares of Microsoft, Oracle, CoreWeave and other AI infra providers have been weak since early-June"; the third-order lever that customers fund token overruns by **optimising elsewhere in their Azure/AWS spend**; and the sourcing-side quotes ("costs are ballooning… IT'S STILL RELATIVELY EARLY," "consumption pricing is one of the biggest concerns we have," "we got our first bill and leadership said 'we don't have the money for this'"). **Date note: the running page header reads 26 July 2026 with prices as of 24 Jul — filed as 07-26.** Capex/FCF/balance-sheet legs of the same note routed to [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md), not duplicated here. Cross-filed [[MSFT]], [[GOOG]], [[AMZN]], [[OPENAI]], [[ANTHROPIC]].
- **2026-07-27** — **/wiki-ingest (Outlook + Twitter corpus). 3 State-of-play bullets. No canonical number superseded.** **The most valuable addition is MS's generational token-margin ladder, which gives this page a supply-side cost curve it lacked: BLACKWELL TOKEN-SALE MARGINS ~58-60%, RUBIN ~75-80%, FEYNMAN ~90% — and the implication stated directly, that 'moving from Blackwell to Feynman could allow TOKEN PRICES TO FALL APPROXIMATELY 75% WHILE MAINTAINING EQUIVALENT MARGINS.' That is the mechanism by which price deflation and margin expansion coexist, and it should anchor how this page reads future price cuts.** **On the demand side, MS puts a number on the headroom argument: MEDIAN CURRENT ENTERPRISE TOKEN SPEND IS LESS THAN $11 PER USER PER MONTH, and a typical AI-assisted enterprise task generates ~$55 OF LABOR SAVINGS FOR ONLY $2-5 OF TOKEN EXPENSE (5-7 agents, ~15x more tokens per agent than a basic query, 375-525K total tokens, $3-10 per million tokens).** **The most decision-relevant number this run is MS's sizing of the Kimi-K3 shock, because the market traded it hard: 'K3 DOES NOT APPEAR TO BE A LOW-COMPUTE MIRACLE… running K3 in a 200 MW facility IMPROVES MODELED MARGINS ONLY APPROXIMATELY 1-3 POINTS versus the prior frontier-model assumption.' A 1-3 point facility-level margin effect against a memory complex that repriced 5-15% is the clearest statement that the selloff over-extrapolated — and MS's broader read is that Chinese open models are 'MORE NEGATIVE FOR US FRONTIER-LAB ECONOMICS THAN FOR INFRASTRUCTURE DEMAND', with Jevons expanding inference usage.** Independent corroboration from the corpus: **K3 hit #10 on OpenRouter at ~140B tokens/day within two days WITH SERVING INFRASTRUCTURE DEGRADING (throughput 30 → 13 tok/s, latency up to ~72s) — the observable first-order effect of the 'efficient' model was capacity failing to keep up.** **Logged as the genuine counter-signal rather than buried: the WSJ reports 'US companies flipped from TOKENMAXXING to THRIFT-MAXXING.' Low absolute spend and deliberate frugality are not contradictory, but this is a first-party report on buyer BEHAVIOUR against MS's model of buyer ECONOMICS, and the behavioural direction is the opposite of what the Jevons argument assumes.** Pricing marker: **[[ANTHROPIC]]'s Claude Opus 5 'comes close to Fable 5 performance at half the price', priced at $5/1M input and $25/1M output — the SAME as Opus 4.8, i.e. a capability step at unchanged list price, which cuts effective cost per unit of work without a headline price cut.** Cross-filed [[NVDA]], [[ANTHROPIC]], [[OPENAI]], [[MSFT]], [themes/hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md).
- **2026-07-26 (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 6 State-of-play bullets. Nothing superseded.** **This page finally has the number it needed: same-benchmark all-in cost, not sticker price. JPM published the total dollar cost to run the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index — Fable 5 $5,631 · Opus 4.8 $3,753 · Kimi-K3 $2,710 · qwen3.7-plus $311 · deepseek-v4-flash $74 — because "models consume very different token volumes to reach the same benchmark, i.e. to complete the same task."** On that basis Fable 5 costs **~2.1x Kimi-K3, ~18x qwen3.7-plus, ~76x deepseek-v4-flash.** Sticker rates for the record (7/21/26): **Fable 5 $10/$50 · Opus 4.8 ~$5/$25 · gpt-5.6-sol ~$5/$30 (≤272K) · Grok 4.5 $2/$6**; **Sonnet 5's introductory $2/$10 reverts to $3/$15 on 9/1/26** (~50% effective increase); **Opus 5.0 shipped 7/24/26 at $5/$25.** **Second, and it cuts against the consensus narrative: on Epoch AI's ECI score the top-3 open-weight vs top-3 closed-weight performance gap has been "relatively stable over the last couple of years, rather than materially converging" — CONTRARY to "the popular investor perception." Open weights are cheaper, not catching up.** **⚠️ Third, the rebuttal that may collapse most of the apparent gap, and it comes with a first-person measurement from a house bearish nearly everything else in AI: Redburn's Haissl argues cost-per-task not sticker, and reports that in his OWN Claude Code usage "stored-cache reads were ~98% of tokens, with fresh input under 2%"; open-weight models also "repeat failed tasks… while frontier models don't"; so they are "only 2x cheaper when you adjust for cache."** The reconciliation worth noticing: **JPM's own Fable-5-vs-Kimi-K3 all-in ratio is ~2.1x — essentially Redburn's "2x"** — so the two houses may agree on the frontier-vs-Kimi gap and **the real dispute is the CHEAP TAIL.** If so, the frontier labs' exposure is not to Kimi-class models but to the very cheap tier absorbing high-volume simple work — which is exactly what routing is built to do. **Fourth: federated routing + SLMs are now "table stakes," with named implementations — Zoom (Z-Scorer), MSFT Foundry Model Router + an explicit small-model family, Intuit GenOS (Claude + GPT), Salesforce Agentforce, Cloudflare AI Gateway (monetizing routing directly), and Snowflake/ServiceNow/Adobe/Oracle on customer-directed model choice; plus ServiceNow's own open-weight reasoning model and the line of the week, "people are realizing you don't need to take a Ferrari to mail a letter."** JPM's framing: additions to the arsenal, not replacements, and collectively the reason software GM compression "can moderate." **Fifth: down-tiering as OBSERVED behaviour — WSJ reports CTOs "utilizing lower-cost, open-source models from China for most tasks and reserving only the most specialized and complex queries for OpenAI and Anthropic."** Note this is compatible with frontier revenue growth **if** the retained queries are the valuable ones — unresolved, and the same question behind the 07-22 UBS and 07-24 Rothschild ARR datapoints. UBS's own four-part read: frontier and open "could both win," GPU demand **reallocated not reduced ("bullish Nvidia")**, hyperscaler risk "more from the high frontier lab exposure," and **"investors are now on edge for any update on OpenAI and Anthropic growth."** **Sixth, the demand side made concrete: AT&T at ~1tn+ tokens/month / ~16.4tn annualized, >100 GenAI models in production (~40% on AMD), >300k calls transcribed daily; SAP describing "the token effect" as a P&L line it charges back to functions while "introducing model routing technologies"; UNH at $1.5B of AI spend in '26; GOOGL at 83% weekly sales-team Gemini usage for up to 20% higher win rates; SHOP with AI-search traffic converting ~50% better at 14% higher order values, traffic +8x and orders +13x y/y.** Cross-filed [[ANTHROPIC]], [[OPENAI]], [[MSFT]], [[GOOG]], [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md).

(2026-07-24)** — the inference moat has MOVED from single-node CUDA to **disaggregated prefill/decode + WideEP**; active experts stay 4-10 while total experts scale 128→512 with MLA-style KV collapse; EP8→EP64 cuts experts/GPU 32→4; NVIDIA reports **up to 2.28x higher per-GPU output throughput from Wide-EP**; ~290MB KV transfer per 8,192-token DeepSeek-R1 FP8-KV prefill. Read: unit token cost is now set at the cluster/topology layer, not the chip layer.
